Chapter 7Digital forensics in next-generation internet of medical things for sustainable telehealthcare

Mariappan Ramasamy

DOI: 10.1201/9781003640325-7

7.1 Introduction

In recent years, the Internet of Things (IoT) technology has received a lot of attention among researchers to explore various applications including healthcare [1]. In order to enhance quality of life and support medical emergencies, the IoT has been utilized recently in wireless sensor networks and in a variety of healthcare applications, including wearable body space networks. In order to monitor heart rate and other characteristics, wearable sensor devices that are connected to the internet can record data like photoplethysmography, EEGs, and ECGs. The Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) is transforming healthcare by connecting medical devices and applications through the internet. This interconnected network allows for seamless data exchange and real-time monitoring, leading to improved diagnostics, personalized treatments, and better patient outcomes. The IoMT is seen to be essential for medical applications, and in order to create a dependable platform, it is essential to enhance and examine every aspect of the IoT infrastructure [2]. The term IoMT [3] describes how medical systems and devices are connected via IoT technologies. It makes remote healthcare, biological signal tracking, disease diagnosis, and patient health monitoring possible without the need for human intervention. The IoMT plays a critical role in healthcare by enhancing patient health and revolutionizing the healthcare sector [4]. It builds an ecosystem of connected medical objects by utilizing smart sensors, smart devices, and lightweight communication protocols [5]. Promising opportunities exist for the development of IoT-based healthcare technology in terms of social effect, economic advantages, and technological improvements. The IoMT transforms [6, 7] healthcare by utilizing IoT technology to improve patient care and medical services. The interconnection of different medical devices/sensors forms an IOMT and is shown in Figure 7.1.

7.2 Digital forensics

Digital forensics (DF) is a fascinating and crucial field that focuses on the recovery, investigation, and analysis of information found in digital devices and networks [8, 9] often in the context of cybercrimes or security breaches. It plays a pivotal role in modern law enforcement, cybersecurity, and legal investigations [10]. DF involves collecting, preserving, and analyzing electronic data to support investigations and legal proceedings. It includes several subdisciplines such as computer forensics, focusing on computers and storage devices, network forensics analyzing network traffic, mobile device forensics, and forensic data analysis [11]. Typically, it follows steps like identifying evidence, acquiring data, analyzing it systematically, and presenting findings in a clear, legally admissible manner. It is used in solving cybercrimes, corporate investigations, fraud detection, and incident response. The life cycle of DF is shown in Figure 7.2.

As shown in Figure 7.2, the DF life cycle [12] typically involves identification, preservation, analysis, documentation, and presentation of digital evidence to support investigations.

  1. Identification: This stage involves identifying the relevant devices, data sources, and potential evidence related to the incident or investigation.
  2. Preservation: Once identified, the next step is to preserve the integrity and authenticity of the evidence by creating copies or images of the data in a secure manner, ensuring no tampering or alteration occurs.
  3. Analysis: This involves examining the collected data to identify relevant information, patterns, and anomalies that can help determine the cause and scope of the incident or crime.
  4. Documentation: Thoroughly documenting all steps taken during the investigation, including evidence collection, analysis methods, and findings, is crucial for legal admissibility and future reference.
  5. Presentation: Finally, the findings and evidence are presented in a clear and concise manner, often in the form of reports or presentations, to relevant stakeholders, such as law enforcement or legal teams.

7.2.1 Classifications of digital forensics

DF is a vast field with several specialized branches, each focusing on different types of digital evidence. The different types of DF [13] are shown in Figure 7.3. The following list shows the different types of DF.

  1. Computer forensics: Focuses on recovering and analyzing data from computers, laptops, and storage devices such as hard drives, USBs, etc. It is commonly used in criminal investigations and data breaches.
  2. Network forensics: Involves monitoring and analyzing network traffic to identify intrusions, attacks, or unauthorized activities. It is crucial for cybersecurity and tracking cybercriminals.
  3. Mobile device forensics: Deals with extracting and examining data from mobile devices, such as smartphones /tablets, including messages, call logs, and app data.
  4. Database forensics: Examines databases and the related metadata to uncover unauthorized activities, such as data tampering or breaches.
  5. Forensic Data Analysis (FDA): Focuses on identifying patterns, anomalies, and trends within large data sets, often used to detect financial fraud or insider threats.
  6. Memory forensics: Analyzes a computer’s volatile memory (RAM) to uncover details about running processes, malware, or other in-memory artifacts.
  7. Cloud forensics: Investigates data stored on cloud platforms to trace cyber incidents and identify evidence.

7.2.2 Applications of digital forensics

DF is indispensable in the modern digital age, ensuring accountability, security, and justice across diverse fields [14]. It has a wide range of applications across various sectors. The various applications of DF are listed below.

  1. Law enforcement: Investigating cybercrimes, such as hacking, identity theft, online fraud, recovering deleted or hidden evidence from digital devices to solve crimes, tracing illegal activities on the dark web.
  2. Corporate investigations: Detecting employee misconduct, such as intellectual property theft or policy violations, investigating financial fraud, embezzlement, analyzing insider threats or data breaches in organizations.
  3. Cybersecurity: Identifying vulnerabilities and sources of attacks on IT systems, analyzing malware to understand its origin and impact, and conducting incident response to mitigate and prevent security breaches.
  4. Legal proceedings: Collecting and analyzing digital evidence for court cases, ensuring that digital evidence complies with legal and regulatory standards, reconstructing timelines and events for use in litigation.
  5. Tele healthcare: In telehealth, specific forensic techniques are employed to ensure the security, integrity, and reliability of digital evidence, investigating breaches or unauthorized access to telehealth systems and protecting medical records and sensitive information from tampering.
  6. Insurance and financial fraud: Applications include uncovering fraudulent claims in insurance processes, detecting unauthorized transactions or money laundering activities, providing digital evidence for auditing purposes.
  7. Military and national security: Investigating cyber warfare and espionage activities, securing classified information and communication networks, monitoring and analyzing cyber threats to national infrastructure.
  8. Education and research: Supporting academic research on DF methodologies, educating future forensic analysts and law enforcement professionals, developing innovative tools and techniques for forensic investigations.

7.2.3 Digital forensics tools

DF tools are hardware and software tools [15] that can be used to aid in the recovery and preservation of digital evidence. Law enforcement can use DF tools to collect and preserve digital evidence and support or refute hypotheses before courts. The various types of DF tools are depicted in Figure 7.4, which includes the Sleuth Kit, Wireshark, Autopsy, SIFT, Paladin, Xplico, ExifTool, and Magnet RAM Capture, etc. Forensic imaging of the systems being investigated, image processing, and producing a report with the information gathered are all steps in the forensic analysis process. Security analysts and investigators can gather forensic data from computer devices, transform it into common formats for study, and filter it to find pertinent information with the aid of DF technologies.

DF tools are essential for investigating and analyzing electronic devices and digital data. They help forensic experts extract, preserve, and analyze evidence in legal cases, cybersecurity incidents, and more. Table 7.1 shows DF tools, known for their capabilities in data analysis, incident response, and evidence collection. Each tool has its unique features and areas of expertise. Choosing the right DF tool depends on several factors such as type of investigation, the devices involved, specific goals, etc.

Table 7.1 Digital forensic tools⏎
ToolCategory Tool Name Description
Disk imaging and data acquisition FTK Imager Creates forensic images of storage devices, preserving data integrity
— EnCase A commercial platform for data acquisition, analysis, and reporting
— Cellebrite UFED A software application for collecting, preserving, and analyzing digital evidence from various devices
Forensic analysis and investigation Autopsy An open-source platform for analyzing disk images and conducting forensic investigations
— Forensic Toolkit (FTK) A commercial digital forensics platform for analyzing and investigating digital evidence
— Magnet AXIOM A digital investigation platform for obtaining and analyzing forensic data
— X-Ways Forensics A tool for analyzing digital evidence and recovering data
Network forensics Wireshark A network protocol analyzer for capturing and examining network traffic
— Xplico A network forensic analysis tool designed to extract data from network traffic
— NetworkMiner A network forensic tool for analyzing network traffic and identifying devices
Other tools Volatility A tool for analyzing memory images
— Registry Recon A tool for extracting and analyzing Windows registry hives
— SIFT Workstation A virtual appliance containing a suite of forensic tools

7.3 Digital forensics in IoMT

The IoMT has revolutionized healthcare by connecting medical devices, sensors, and systems, enabling real-time monitoring, efficient diagnostics, and personalized treatments [16]. However, this interconnected ecosystem also introduces significant challenges in security, privacy, and DF. DF in the IoMT is a critical aspect of ensuring the security and integrity of connected medical devices and the sensitive data they handle. The need for security/ privacy/DF n IoMT is shown in Figure 7.5. Since patient EHRs are currently one of the primary targets for malevolent attackers, investigators must also ensure data owners’ privacy throughout the investigative process [17]. Three steps are suggested for DF because IoT is primarily cloud-based. Users’ devices come first, followed by the network over which the data are transferred, and lastly, cloud servers. The chain of custody must be followed in order for any digital evidence that is retrieved to be trustworthy, genuine, comprehensive, credible, and admissible before it can be used as evidence in court.

  1. Incident response: When data breaches or system compromises occur, DF helps identify the source of the attack, the methods used, and the extent of the damage.
  2. Data protection and compliance: Ensuring that sensitive patient information is handled in compliance with regulations like HIPAA or GDPR. Forensics can verify that no unauthorized access or tampering has occurred.
  3. Malware detection: Identifying and analyzing malware that may target telehealth platforms, such as ransomware or spyware, to prevent future incidents.
  4. Auditing and monitoring: Analyzing system logs and network activity to detect anomalies, unauthorized access, or suspicious activities in real time.
  5. Cloud and remote access forensics: Investigating breaches in cloud systems that store medical records or telehealth data. It ensures that remote consultations are secure and free from interference.
  6. Device and wearable forensics: Examining telehealth devices, such as health monitors and wearables, for signs of tampering or misuse, ensuring the accuracy and authenticity of health data.
  7. Fraud detection: Identifying fraudulent activities, such as false insurance claims or unauthorized use of telehealth services, using forensic techniques.

The three-tiered architecture of DF using device/things forensics, network forensics and cloud forensics is shown in Figure 7.6.

Apart from DF, AI/ML techniques [18] are transforming the healthcare industry by enhancing clinical diagnostics, healthcare management, and public health infrastructures. Drug development, disease diagnosis, and effective hospital resource utilization are just a few of the healthcare uses of AI and ML. By strengthening their abilities and promoting patient outcomes, service quality, and the overall effectiveness of the healthcare system, AI and ML has the potential to support healthcare providers, and thereby, it can save costs and improve everyone’s health. Large volumes of healthcare data can be analyzed by AI and ML, which can also spot trends and help with activities like image processing and repetitive task automation. Moreover, AI/ML techniques can facilitate natural language processing, which makes it easier for medical professionals to access and analyze patient data through natural language interface. Though AI and ML are at their early stage, they have potential applications in IoMT for medical therapeutics in near future. Wireless body sensors use ML techniques like support vector machines and decision trees to gather healthcare data using smart technologies [19]. These ML methods help in the diagnosis and prognosis of diseases in order to provide highly accurate predictions and classifications. For later usage, the processed data will be kept on cloud storage.

The layered architecture of IoMT is shown in Figure 7.7. The biomedical sensors, wireless sensors, and recorded data are represented by the perception layer. The gateway layer is in charge of moving the gathered data to a long-range or short-range communication network, where it is stored in a network server or IoTs server at the data storage layer. Finally, the application layer, which is an application server that runs user applications, monitors, controls, etc. with the aid of cloud, edge, or fog computing, allows access to or visualization of the stored data. The healthcare technology has greatly evolved with the integration of AI/ML techniques into IoMT for data management [20]. Massive volumes of patient data are produced by IoMT devices and saved in electronic health records (EHRs), including vital signs and medication adherence records.

AI/ML techniques can help medical practitioners and healthcare providers with early disease detection, appropriate treatment regimens, and predictive healthcare by analyzing, interpreting, and extracting useful features from IoMT data and thereby meeting the demands of each patient. Furthermore, protocols that include ML into IoMT increase decision support systems, expedite diagnosis, and constantly improve health. However, as sensitive medical data serves as the foundation for well-informed medical decisions and customized treatment plans, this integration also places a high priority. The healthcare system may be completely transformed by including data processing and machine learning (ML) into the IoMT by enabling remote healthcare system.

7.5 Machine learning in telehealthcare

Machine learning (ML) is significantly enhancing the IoMT for telemedicine, leading to improved healthcare delivery and patient outcomes. The integration of ML within the IoMT has significantly enhanced telemedicine applications, enabling improved patient monitoring and disease prediction [24]. These advancements are transforming telemedicine by providing real-time patient data, improving diagnostics, and enabling personalized treatments. The integration of ML in IoMT for telehealthcare is shown in Figure 7.9.

The three tiers of traditional telemedicine are:

  1. Tier 1: Thing layer (wireless (body) sensors)
  2. Tier 2: Network layer (fog) gateway, and
  3. Tier 3: Data management layer/ML layer

The hospital/healthcare is fundamentally a component of the cloud computing infrastructure and telemedicine system. Given that the client is represented by Tiers 1 and 2 of remote healthcare monitoring, the server is represented by Tier 3.

Tier 1: Thing layer (edge) for data collection

Vital signs for personal healthcare are gathered from patients’ gadgets in this tier. These medical devices have a variety of sensors that track physiological parameters like blood pressure and ECGs. Every sensor transmits a signal, which is then processed to yield the outcomes and the following data collected from various sensors into the pool.

  1. Blood pressure sensor
  2. SPO2 sensor
  3. ECG/EEG/EMG sensors
  4. Temperature sensor

Tier 2: Network layer (fog) for data communication

After receiving the data from the previous layer, this layer alerts the user’s designated personal devices. Additionally, using an external gateway that enables long-distance connection, this layer is in charge of routinely transmitting data to the distant server. Since the gateway connects the public access network to the home networks, data are sent to a central server or hospital by the gateway’s components.

Tier 3: Data management/ML layer/processing/storage layer

The last layer is where the ML/data processing and storage process is carried out. Sensors and server-based devices are among the medical sources in the system. Real-time data delivery is provided. Doctors can, however, examine data in real time by utilizing the remote control. This layer may have cloud for data storage and infrastructure for processing.

ML layer:

This ML layer is used to analyze large volumes of real-time medical data collected from connected devices, identify patterns, predict potential health risks, and provide insights to healthcare providers for better decision-making and personalized patient care; essentially, it leverages ML algorithms to extract meaningful information from the raw data, enabling proactive healthcare management and improved patient outcomes.

Application layer:

The application layer in IoMT is primarily used for data transmission and processing of medical information collected from connected medical devices, enabling features like real-time patient monitoring, remote patient care, early disease detection, and facilitating communication between medical devices and healthcare systems; essentially, it allows for the practical application [25] of the data gathered by IoMT devices through functionalities like data analysis, alerts, and integration with healthcare platforms.

Applications:

  1. Remote monitoring/diagnosis: ML algorithms analyze data from IoMT devices to diagnose diseases remotely. This allows healthcare providers to offer timely and accurate diagnoses without the need for in-person visits.
  2. Patient monitoring: IoMT devices equipped with ML continuously monitor patients’ vital signs and health metrics. These data are analyzed in real time to detect anomalies and alert healthcare providers, ensuring prompt intervention.
  3. Personalized treatment: ML tailors treatment plans based on individual patient data collected from IoMT devices. This personalized approach improves the effectiveness of treatments and enhances patient care.
  4. Emergency response: ML-based telemedicine frameworks prioritize remote patients with chronic diseases for emergency healthcare services. This ensures that patients with urgent needs receive timely medical attention.
  5. Data security: ML enhances the security of IoMT systems by detecting and mitigating cyber threats, ensuring that patient data is handled securely and in compliance with privacy regulations.

These advancements facilitate timely medical interventions and personalized healthcare solutions. However, several limitations [26] persist that hinder the full realization of these technologies.

Limitations and challenges of machine learning in IoMT

While the potential of ML in IoMT for telemedicine is substantial, addressing these limitations is crucial for enhancing the effectiveness and accessibility of healthcare solutions. Future research should focus on developing cost-effective strategies and improving data security measures to overcome these challenges [27].

  1. Data privacy concerns: The integration of IoMT raises significant issues regarding patient data security and privacy, necessitating robust encryption and regulatory compliance.
  2. Implementation barriers: Socioeconomic factors, such as high costs and technological access in less-developed regions, limit the widespread adoption of these systems.
  3. Device reliability: Challenges related to sensor accuracy and data transmission speed can affect the reliability of health monitoring systems.
  4. Interoperability: Diverse healthcare devices and systems often lack compatibility, complicating data sharing and integration.
  5. Energy consumption: Many IoMT devices are constrained by battery life, which can limit the deployment of complex ML algorithms.
  6. Limited data availability: Insufficient data can hinder the training of effective ML models, leading to biased or inaccurate predictions.
  7. Cloud Integration: The combination of IoMT with cloud computing enhances data storage and processing capabilities, facilitating better patient interactions and diagnostic accuracy.

While the potential of ML in IoMT for telemedicine is substantial, addressing these challenges is crucial for enhancing the effectiveness and accessibility of healthcare solutions. Future research should focus on developing cost-effective strategies and improving data security measures to overcome these challenges [28]. Implementing ML in the IoMT for telemedicine presents several challenges and limitations that must be addressed to enhance healthcare delivery. These challenges include data security, privacy concerns, interoperability, and the need for efficient algorithms capable of handling large volumes of data [29].

Addressing the challenges

  • Enhanced security protocols: Implementing advanced cybersecurity measures, such as data encryption and two-factor authentication, can mitigate risks.
  • Development of novel architectures: Exploring new ML architectures and optimization techniques can improve model efficiency and adaptability in low-resource environments.
  • Standardization efforts: Promoting interoperability standards among IoMT devices can facilitate seamless data exchange and integration.

7.7 AI ethics

In the context of IoMT and DF, ethical considerations are paramount, encompassing data privacy, patient confidentiality, and the responsible use of collected evidence. The following is the ethical considerations [33] for DF in IoMT.

  1. Data privacy and patient confidentiality
    • Protecting sensitive information: IoMT devices often collect and transmit highly sensitive medical data, making privacy a critical concern. Digital forensic investigations must prioritize the protection of this data, ensuring it’s handled ethically and legally.
    • Minimizing data collection: Forensic specialists should only collect the data necessary for the investigation, avoiding unnecessary data collection and storage.
    • Anonymization and pseudonymization: Techniques like anonymization or pseudonymization should be employed to protect patient identities when sharing or analyzing data.
    • Data security: Robust security measures are essential to prevent unauthorized access, modification, or disclosure of sensitive medical data during storage, transmission, and analysis.
  2. Legal and ethical challenges
    • Compliance with laws and regulations: Digital forensic investigations must adhere to all applicable laws/regulations, including data protection laws like HIPAA (US) and GDPR (Europe).
    • Obtaining consent: When possible, obtaining informed consent from patients or their legal representatives before conducting digital forensic investigations is crucial.
    • Chain of custody: Maintaining a clear and verifiable chain of custody for digital evidence is essential to ensure its integrity and admissibility in legal proceedings.
    • Transparency and accountability: Forensic specialists should be transparent about their methods and findings, and accountable for their actions during the investigation.
    • Cross-border investigations: IoMT devices can be used across different jurisdictions, which can lead to legal and jurisdictional challenges when conducting forensic investigations.
  3. Ethical principles for DF
    • Integrity: Maintaining the integrity of digital evidence is paramount, ensuring that it remains unaltered and reliable.
    • Objectivity: Forensic specialists should approach investigations objectively, without bias or preconceived notions.
    • Confidentiality: Protecting the confidentiality of sensitive information is a fundamental ethical principle.
    • Competence: Forensic specialists should possess the necessary skills and knowledge to conduct investigations effectively and ethically.
    • Honesty: Forensic specialists should be honest and transparent in their findings and reporting.

7.8 Current trends and future challenges

DF in the IoMT faces challenges including data security, privacy concerns, device heterogeneity, limited processing power, and the need for specialized tools and procedures to collect and analyze data from diverse medical devices and cloud environments. It faces challenges including data collection from diverse and resource-constrained devices, data integrity and privacy concerns, and the need for specialized expertise and tools. The current trends in DF, shaping the field in 2025 are listed below. The future trends in DF in IoMT are depicted in Figure 7.12.

  1. AI and machine learning integration: Artificial intelligence is being used to automate forensic processes, analyze large datasets, and identify patterns or anomalies more efficiently. This reduces manual workload and enhances accuracy.
  2. Cloud forensics: With the growing reliance on cloud storage, forensic experts are developing specialized tools to investigate data breaches and recover evidence from distributed cloud environments.
  3. Mobile and IoT forensics: As mobile devices and IoT devices become more prevalent, forensic techniques are adapting to extract and analyze data from these sources, including wearables and smart home devices.
  4. Deepfake detection: The rise of deepfake technology has led to the development of tools to identify and analyze manipulated media, ensuring the authenticity of digital evidence.
  5. Blockchain forensics: With the increasing use of cryptocurrencies, blockchain forensics is gaining importance in tracking transactions and uncovering financial crimes.
  6. Quantum-resistant forensics: As quantum computing advances, forensic methods are being updated to address potential threats to encryption and data security.
  7. Remote forensics: The shift to remote work has increased the need for forensic tools that can investigate incidents without physical access to devices or networks.
  8. Ethical AI and data privacy: Forensic practices are focusing on maintaining ethical standards and ensuring compliance with data privacy regulations during investigations.

These trends highlight the dynamic nature of DF and its critical role in addressing modern cybersecurity challenges. While these challenges are significant, they also present opportunities for innovation in telemedicine. Addressing these limitations can lead to more effective and secure healthcare solutions, ultimately improving patient outcomes.

  1. Data acquisition and collection
    • Diverse devices: IoMT encompasses a wide range of devices with varying capabilities and data formats, making it difficult to develop a universal forensic approach.
    • Resource constraints: Many IoMT devices have limited storage, processing power, and connectivity, making data collection and analysis challenging.
    • Data volatility: IoMT devices often store data temporarily or in volatile memory, which can be lost or modified easily, requiring rapid data acquisition and preservation.
    • Data format diversity: IoMT devices use various data formats, including sensor data, images, and logs, which can be difficult to extract and analyze.
    • Data volume: The sheer volume of data generated by IoMT devices can be overwhelming, requiring efficient data collection and storage techniques.
  2. Specific challenges in IoMT forensics
    • Device diversity: IoMT encompasses a wide range of devices, each with its own unique characteristics and data storage mechanisms, posing challenges for forensic analysis.
    • Real-time data: IoMT devices often generate real-time data, requiring forensic specialists to develop techniques for capturing and analyzing this data in a timely and accurate manner.
    • Interoperability: IoMT devices may communicate with various systems and devices, requiring forensic specialists to understand the interoperability aspects of these systems.
    • Lack of standards: The IoMT field is relatively new, and there are still gaps in standards and best practices for DF.
  3. Data integrity and chain of custody
    • Data alteration: IoMT devices are susceptible to data tampering and modification, making it crucial to ensure the integrity of collected evidence.
    • Chain of custody: Establishing a clear chain of custody for evidence collected from IoMT devices is essential for legal admissibility.
    • Data synchronization: IoMT devices often synchronize data with cloud services, making it difficult to determine the origin and authenticity of data.
  4. Specialized expertise and tools
    • Lack of skilled professionals: There is a shortage of forensic professionals with the expertise to conduct IoMT investigations.
    • Need for specialized tools: IoMT forensic investigations require specialized tools and techniques that are not readily available.
    • Training and education: Forensic professionals need to be trained on the latest IoMT technologies, forensic techniques and tools.
  5. Ethics, privacy and compliance: IoMT devices often collect sensitive patient data, requiring strict adherence to privacy regulations such as HIPAA/GDPR. Also, techniques for anonymizing patient data to protect privacy while allowing forensic analysis are needed. IoMT forensics must comply with relevant regulations and standards, such as those related to data security and privacy.

7.9 Conclusion

In this book chapter, the digital Forensic techniques are applied to enhance personalized healthcare systems within the framework of the IoMT towards smart Tele-healthcare. Since AI and ML fosters trust and guarantees decision-making authority, it is regarded as significant in healthcare applications. The use of DF in IoMT has facilitated the development of AI/ML methods for robotic surgery, personalized treatment, prediction methods, and the diagnosis of cardiac disease. In the context of IoMT, digital forensic techniques are used to improve customized healthcare systems. These DF techniques will help the healthcare providers/physicians to make the transparent and best decision in telehealthcare system. Hence, DF is much impactful in tele-healthcare applications because it ensures integrity, confidentiality, and availability of healthcare/personal records and builds confidence. Hence, DF techniques in IoMT are analyzed to telemedicine applications such as robotic surgery, personalized health care, prediction techniques, and cardiac disease detection.

Modern digital evidence problems have become complex, leading investigators to improve their investigative process by integrating artificial intelligence (AI) technology into forensic systems. Criminal investigators encounter digital evidence in their work more often, so they introduce modern systems for their investigations Investigation teams must sustain security measures for evidence through established digital evidence procedures, which enable appropriate findings before the court requests investigation materials because of progressing digital technology. According to Ref [1], forensic investigators must arrange the elements of a digital database chronologically when they need to show specific incidents. During the last decade, investigators used forensic-led principle-based digital investigation systems, which helped technology achieve unprecedented advances. The primary technology achievement in addressing major cybercrime operations belongs to software analysis development. The development of digital forensic technology advances because AI and machine learning speed up system data processing for evidence acquisition [2].

Data science analytics monitoring systems reach success points when social network systems integrate with author identification bodies, according to the findings of [3]. Organisations reach their investigative levels with the help of expert data analysis methods when such techniques help probable cause investigations find new patterns. AI delivers fast-resolution methods for handling size-related problems that appear during investigations of extensive electronic evidence in digital forensics cases. The computer software needs predefined development programs that address all operational data types. Big data analysis techniques like MapReduce allow modern developers to improve law enforcement cases using AI algorithms that examine evidence from databases, network information, and digital photo assets. The innovation delivered two key advantages for evidence verification operations and expedient digital evidence processing. The advanced system executes human relational studies through its well-defined operational functions and writes pattern matching to detect authors during source validation operations. According to Ref [4], the police gained positive results from their forensic examinations with the support of dependable AI solutions.

Further, the IoMT led to a fundamental transformation of healthcare systems by enhancing the investigation of medical information and the methods of delivering healthcare treatment and control capabilities. Medical facilities built an extensive network comprising multiple security points of vulnerability. Healthcare digital innovation emerged by merging the Internet of Medical Things smartphones and smart devices with cloud services, enabling access to healthcare treatments that boost patient health outcomes [5]. The more extended deployment of IoMT devices leads to accelerated device interconnectivity and exposes possible security vulnerabilities to hackers [6].

IoMT technology delivers three fundamental advantages to healthcare: pandemic management, real-time supervision, and personalised medical services. IoT devices and their related data have become prevalent throughout networks to present total solutions for resolving the associated security challenges. Medical experts [7] explain how strong response protocols in IoT-generated monitoring systems are necessary due to emerging vaccine-resistant strains. Edge devices connect to healthcare data infrastructure, escalating security exposure to sensitive health information because of malicious programs, ransomware breaches, and data theft acts, as described by [8]. Security measurement elevation must advance to work toward monitoring all challenges facing the complete IoMT system environment.

Security challenges found in IoMT systems stem from three basic forms: faulty devices combined with insecure data transfer protocols and unacceptable data storage systems. A proper solution emerges from hardware remediation, improved software stability, authentication upgrades, encryption control, and software updates. Protecting the environment requires two essential components: safe communication protocols and intrusion detection systems. At its best, patient care requires every strategic plan component to be implemented effectively [9].

The Healthcare transformation occurs through IoMT, which connects devices to sensors that generate health process information before guiding this information in the proper direction. According to Ref [10], medical diagnosis performance increases in the digital healthcare ecosystem because its electronic delivery system improves operational efficiency and supports better remote care monitoring and superior healthcare diagnostics. Strong forensic methods must be implemented to enable widespread IoMT deployment because security threats and data tampering appear while adopting this technology [11]. Cyberattacks against IoMT equipment and their linked networks continue to increase, so healthcare providers must immediately establish security measures alongside incident response systems. Unique forensic technology requires immediate development because real-time data transmission interacts with privacy regulations that define the IoMT technological setting [12].

Through its integration with IoMT forensic operations, AI creates solutions that provide operational performance improvements and highly precise results at scale. Several AI systems unite to detect irregularities and speed up evidence acquisition while conducting data analysis [13]. To handle security incident reconstruction processes, the system performs fast data processing that acts crucially in breach investigation and cyber assault response management stages. AI implementation within IoMT performs two vital security operations: danger discovery and threat response guidance. Prediction-based vulnerability assessments through the system detect platform weaknesses in devices and network and data system vulnerabilities. AI develops robust security structures that use real-time control performance assessment to create established procedural systems. Security protocols applied by these systems provide primary defences that protect patient data and devices used in IoMT [14].

Synthetic intelligence methods now detect top-level cyber threats by combining machine learning, deep learning, and natural language processing systems. When dealing with advanced persistent threats, AI-based systems achieve optimal results [15]. Through designed algorithms, management security policies receive recommendations for needed security control adjustments to handle current threats. Security systems using AI technology automatically detect how to adapt their digital surveillance capabilities without changing staffing expenses. The Random Forest algorithm is a machine learning technology that develops self-protecting security alerts for defending assets from active cyberattacks while providing unknown threat identification capabilities [16].

AI-based forensic tools are essential to combat the security vulnerabilities affecting Internet of Medical Things devices and their network infrastructure. AI improves fundamental crime investigation functions because automated anomaly searches within the system lead investigators to discover evidence more rapidly. AI performs two duties: surveying medical devices throughout networks and examining device susceptibilities to preserve medical system security and protect patient privacy. 8.2 AI in IoMT forensic investigations: an overview

AI technology allows forensic science to produce better results during crime scene assessments, evidence confirmation, and suspect investigation procedures. The system achieves peak performance by examining extensive datasets to generate patterns that provide exact results, which create faster investigations [20]. The new digital evidence technologies require complex management capabilities that AI demonstrates a strong potential to handle correctly. Established AI principles connected to digital forensics procedures enable organisations to process information quickly and detect evidence better. US federal entities, state departments, and departmental bodies have authorised AI digital forensics enhancements [21].

AI and automation deployment in forensic applications yield three principal advantages: higher operational excellence, economic resource savings, and decreased error rates. Organisations create higher profits through investments in automation since operational costs remain below the prices of manual human workers [22]. Limited operational budgets show that AI works perfectly for forensic laboratories because it combines economic advantages with enhanced analytical capabilities. When human operational knowledge is combined with AI, it results in exceptional success during crime investigation processes because the system aligns expert skills with established techniques and functional disciplinary understanding. Teamwork between personnel helps increase the detection ability of digital evidence and strengthens cyber network security The research-based analysis of AI operational boundaries needs extensive investigation to establish a complete understanding [23].

AI automation facilitates forensic scientists in tackling complex issues in their available time while automation responsibilities run independently. AI systems gain anomaly recognition capabilities during training, eventually producing reports while labelling evidence and executing work tasks with higher speed [24]. Implementing new efficiency technology diminishes investigative operation time and human error to produce accurate, dependable outcomes. Digital forensic staff leverage automated instruments to perform three basic tasks: collecting evidence, building documentation, and processing evidence during the development of automated analytics solutions. The operational benefits of AI forensic software include two main advantages: enhanced accuracy of trustworthy results and improved specific field performance. Experts use AI techniques in digital evidence assessments to process enormous amounts of digital data quickly and precisely, as described [25].

Automated systems require additional development to convert observed data into textual outputs. ART at maximal capability uses contextual evaluation procedures to extract the exact meanings of digital evidence from details to produce precise outcome analysis. The continuous research in digital forensics develops because digital evidence remains the essential foundation for criminal investigation evidence [26]. Forensic investigation deployments using AI technology reduce case duration while producing superior operational outcomes. Discoveries emerge for forensic technologies through AI technical advances.

Also, AI produces innovations that help users establish quick threat detection methods and produce predictive capabilities above standard security operations. 8.3 Security challenges in IoMT forensics

Medical devices and health systems connect through Internet of Medical Things technology to transmit data for modern healthcare, including remote patient supervision and enhanced care delivery. The extensive interconnected nature of medical devices brings significant security risks that need thorough attention to protect medical data confidentiality, availability, and integrity. IoMT security and privacy challenges are hindered by institutions’ limited ability to monitor components and enforce security policies. At the same time, its network-based device connection systems present data collection and decision-making enhancement capabilities [39]. Understanding these issues will enable the complete realisation of IoMT technology capabilities. The IoMT data contains multiple elements, including patient information and medical records, vital parameters, diagnosis outputs, and sensor measurement results. Multiple healthcare sources, including wearable devices, remote monitoring systems, and electronic health records, send data to cloud storage, which healthcare providers access data security has become more vulnerable because the IoMT advances healthcare data toward complete digitisation and extends its distribution across various locations and its frequent movement. Protecting sensitive data requires priority because privacy concerns are extreme The healthcare system uses patient information from this data to maintain transparency throughout all phases of patient tracking. Smartphones and other smart devices now represent a significant problem for healthcare privacy breaches. IoMT data, with its extensive distribution and complexity, establishes conditions that make security threats highly likely to occur [40].

Security threats within the IoMT data landscape include malware, ransomware data breaches and denial-of-service attacks, which break patient privacy and affect healthcare services while putting lives in danger from network issues and hacking. The practice of third-party control over patient data has revealed security vulnerabilities, according to [41]. Patient data protection becomes more difficult because information volumes rise steadily. A proper solution to combat dangers should use technical solutions, administrative practices, and physical defence methods. A complete security strategy must evaluate vulnerabilities across the entire data management period, from device collection to processing. Properly defending IoMT systems requires multiple protective methods, including robust authentication systems, encryption protocols, and continuous software developments to counter hacking attempts. Healthcare data breaches continue to increase, so healthcare organisations require better approaches to protect their information. Patient security is crucial because healthcare organisations gather sensitive data for network server storage to deliver healthcare services [42]. High trust levels in the sensitive information processing healthcare smartphone network represent the most essential priority. Organisations in healthcare need to meet the standards of HIPAA and GDPR to protect sensitive information and must face penalties when data breaches occur. Healthcare organisations must implement security protocols to give patients the right to access their information while acquiring their permissions for data utilisation.

Medical technology now benefits from the Internet of Things convergence to create the Internet of Medical Things that integrates multiple medical systems for better patient outcomes, operational efficiency, and remote healthcare control. The surge of Internet of Medical Things devices, between wearable fitness trackers and implantable medical devices, has led to massive flows of sensitive patient information that require strong defensive techniques to block improper data access and protect against data breaches and cyberattacks. Advanced digitisation and growing smartphone and smart device diffusion continue to increase the vulnerability landscape [43]. The safety of IoMT devices requires exceptional attention since security breaches can lead to multiple severe consequences, which range from privacy breaches to monetary losses and jeopardised patient safety and may even result in fatalities.

Malware injection attacks target IoMT devices because of their built-in design and configuration weaknesses. Unlawful perpetrators gain control through these attacks to steal sensitive data, disrupting regular device operations. IoMT devices suffer from multiple cyber threats because insufficient security controls allow attackers to launch attacks through weak authentication and encryption. The risk level remains high because IoMT devices connect numerous devices, which allows a single breached device to expose the entire network. The accelerating growth of IoT equipment has generated numerous security weaknesses that create issues for data handling and storage functions. Security precautions need immediate implementation because malware injection attacks against IoMT devices create severe consequences, such as security breaches, device malfunctions, and patient safety risks [44].

IoT device proliferation generates various security vulnerabilities that attract cybercriminals with opportunistic intentions. The inadequate security parameters and different designs, coupled with the restricted functionalities of these devices, make them vulnerable to various threats. IoMT devices attract hackers due to their large quantities of persistent internet connectivity, multiple vulnerabilities, and basic and unsecured setups. The specific development of malicious software targeting such devices resulted in complicated obstacles for existing intrusion detection methods [45]. The protection of IoMT devices has become essential because their adoption rate continues to rise quickly.

The forensic analysis of IoMT requires immediate attention because malware attacks and data breaches affect the limited capabilities of these devices, which operate on various platforms. The extensive data range and undefined network structure make traditional forensics techniques inadequate. Digital investigations become more complicated due to the absence of standard procedures and tools, which forces forensic investigators to produce new methods to handle digital evidence acquisition analysis and interpretation steps [46]. All IoT devices generate essential evidence artefacts that help investigators conduct their work. Investigators must understand undisclosed resources and their strategies to recover artefacts from these devices.

IoMT forensics uses incident response strategies and data collection activities before performing evidence analysis to address data breaches and malware attacks. During incident response, teams must detect security threats; afterwards, they separate them from the systems and eliminate dangers before maintaining evidence integrity. A comprehensive data collection process requires investigators to collaborate to acquire data from IoMT systems and IT infrastructures through multiple methods. Foreign examiners encounter difficulties dealing with the diverse range of IoT devices because of their heterogeneous nature. Evidence analysis involves examining acquired data to detect intrusive codes while following attacker actions until investigators establish the size of the incident impact. The sought objective entails obtaining complete insight into incidents, allowing organisations to implement remediation measures that strengthen their protective measures [47].

Further, the healthcare industry transformed significantly by adopting medical things-connected technology because doctors now monitor patients remotely while creating individual care plans and providing better diagnosis capabilities. The interconnection of various devices has created substantial security risks through which cyber attackers view IoMT devices as desirable targets. Security breaches need comprehensive forensic investigations to help investigators find essential causes, identify damages, and gather legal evidence, as detailed in [48]. The variety of IoMT platforms, data locations, and multiple communication protocols create substantial problems for collecting evidence. To efficiently retrieve and analyse essential information, investigators require extensive knowledge of different device constructs alongside data formats. IoMT systems spread data storage points between device-based storage cloud storage and local server storage, making it hard to locate and obtain full data access [49].

The reduced capabilities of several IoMT devices, such as their limited processing potential and restricted data storage capacities, negatively affect traditional forensic examination methods. The insufficient capabilities of these devices prevent them from running advanced forensic tools and maintaining abundant data storage, which complicates obtaining the entire unmodified device memory snapshot. Network segmentation in IoMT environments is a barrier for forensic investigations because it creates limited access to vital networking surveillance and log recording files. Calamities involving IoMT data result in difficulties when attempting to obtain evidence promptly. The process becomes complicated because different devices communicate with nonstandard protocols and use nonstandard data formats. IoMT devices have become more challenging to investigate because wireless communication depends on their interconnected devices, while encryption and dynamic networks create challenges in analysing network traffic [50].

The poor security measures in IoMT devices create additional obstacles to evidence collection, based on their susceptibility to tampering, lack of reliable logging mechanisms, and inadequate authentication protocols. Protecting evidence coherence alongside proof authenticity stands as the essential priority in the Internet of Medical Things forensic investigations. Thorough protective measures need to be established by investigators to stop unauthorised modifications of evidence. The process meets challenges through its legal obligations and ethical requirements, which require authorisation for patient data access and rule compliance. The investigation process becomes complicated due to the proprietary data formats and physical interfaces while using protocols encountered explicitly in IoMT devices. Digital forensic investigations require solid knowledge of IoMT devices, security systems, and behaviour patterns.

The acquisition process is a vital requirement for forensic investigations because of the difficulties experienced in practising IoMT forensics. A successful approach demands skilled personnel who understand technology and legal requirements and follow industry-best standards. Forensic investigators must create unique tools and analysis methods for IoMT devices and guidelines that control patient data access to achieve success. Investigators must consider the diverse features of different medical internet-of-things devices, including their operational systems and communication methods. Evidence collection demands multiple sources, as evidence must come from sensor devices, communication devices, intelligent systems and smart appliances [51].

8.4 Legal frameworks and compliance in IoMT forensics

Medical data security faces significant obstacles because of the growth of the Internet of Medical Things while enabling the healthcare revolution through technology. Yet, this expansion requires a detailed examination of legal boundaries and adherence to regulations. The shortage of specialised IoMT forensic standards creates substantial obstacles because investigators must use traditional digital methods that inadequately understand specific IoMT system characteristics. IoMT devices encompass diverse equipment that extends from wearable sensors to medical imaging tools, which create difficulties when identifying, collecting, and analysing digital evidence. Jurisdictional uncertainties and evidence inadmissibility issues during legal proceedings become aggravated by unclear definitions between private and public networks. Many investigators have reported additional complexity in computer forensics since the IoMT integrates diverse information through multiple system objects. IoMT forensic operations depend on existing legal standards and compliance requirements because these mechanisms protect patient-sensitive data according to guidelines set by [52].

The convergence of AI with IoMT systems and digital forensics creates healthcare benefits and privacy and security obstacles concerning HIPAA regulations. Healthcare organisations need an extensive framework that safeguards protected health information because IoMT devices create numerous sensitive patient records. In contrast, health institutions use AI more intensively for medical analysis and diagnosis. Healthcare institutions that select AI-driven IoMT solutions must adhere to HIPAA regulations even though these laws did not initially consider such technologies. HIPAA compliance becomes more complex due to AI-driven IoMT system forensic investigations because these investigations need to access patient data subject to legal and ethical restrictions.

Healthcare practitioners find it challenging to use AI-driven IoMT systems to balance data-driven decision-making with privacy protection, handle liability risks and IP issues, and maintain secure privacy standards. Healthcare specialists find it challenging to trust AI systems because of the combined ethical, legal, and regulatory medical framework. At the same time, concerns about algorithmic discrimination and bias also exist alongside clinician refusals to accept AI assistance. A comprehensive solution to these matters needs attention to technical elements and legal and ethical perspectives [53].

Complex algorithms paired with opaque “black box” operations prove difficult for healthcare professionals to understand because they reduce transparency and create accountability issues. Because of insufficient transparency, healthcare professionals face difficulties understanding and validating AI system diagnoses and conclusions. A patient usually seeks to understand the reason behind an AI diagnosis once an automated medical conclusion is discussed. Healthcare providers, alongside their patients, need explainable AI techniques to understand how AI algorithms function per recommendations. Specifically, healthcare providers must adhere to patient data regulation requirements and their technical protocols under HIPAA.

Solutions must address these technical, legal, and ethical system requirements by implementing a comprehensive framework. Medical practitioners are responsible for their choices regardless of whether they use AI algorithm recommendations they understand poorly. Medical professionals who do not utilise AI tool advice or disobey its recommendations during patient care face possible clinical negligence when adverse outcomes occur. Implementing robust security protocols protects IoMT devices and AI systems against cyber dangers and access breaches. Deploying these security measures requires encryption, authentication features, access limitations, and intrusion detection systems [54]. Data anonymisation and deidentification methods help organisations prevent sensitive patient data exposure when performing forensic examinations and training AI models.

To satisfy HIPAA requirements and other applicable rules, healthcare organisations must create detailed guidelines and procedures for AI-driven IoMT system implementations. Healthcare policies need to cover three essential domains: managing health data, incident security procedures, and patient access to monitoring their healthcare information. Healthcare organisations need ethical frameworks to direct the development alongside the deployment of AI technology because this ensures responsible and fair use of these technological systems. Developers should focus more on clinicians when creating AI tools. The solution requires design elements that enhance professional healthcare user clarity and operational ease since clinicians operate medical settings as end-users. The use of AI in healthcare facilities demands attention to privacy matters, confidentiality protection, and healthcare data security, together with proper AI system development practices. Medical professionals need complete information and practical training from doctors regarding AI applications used in healthcare. Patients, alongside healthcare providers, need complete knowledge of AI threats that affect privacy and confidentiality.

The General Data Protection Regulation was introduced as a key data protection framework that drives global organisations to alter their processes, gives users additional control, and modernises legal frameworks alongside consumer trust elevation and administrative burden reduction for companies. The general data protection regulation establishes control mechanisms for customers to protect personal data belonging to European Union residents. Organisations worldwide experience substantial challenges when implementing GDPR because they must deeply understand how this legislation modifies the rules of emerging technologies, mainly AI functions. The European market connections of organisations cause GDPR to affect international markets. Relevant authorities must follow data protection law enforcement directive rules while handling data for their law enforcement activities [55].

GDPR creates complex oversight conditions for international digital forensics regulations through its intricate legal obligations and technical requirements. The analysis of digital evidence through scientific investigative techniques constitutes digital forensics, which remains central to criminal investigations regarding cybercrime, data theft events, and other security cases. International data transfers within digital investigations encounter GDPR limitations because of its strict rules regarding cross-border data movement. The addition of big data creates multiple challenges because it makes it difficult to separate personal information from nonpersonal data, violates rules about data minimisation, and destroys the ability to obtain valid consent. Organisations must understand and follow the combination of GDPR and relevant digital forensics rules from different territories.

Differences between GDPR and digital forensics regulations create implementation challenges because they follow opposing legal approaches and objectives. GDPR maintains privacy protection for individuals as digital forensics aims to investigate crimes through evidence collection. The technical complexity and this asymmetry produce difficulties in upholding individual rights and privacy protection. GDPR presents an obligation for organisations to limit their data collection to what is essential for a particular purpose. The broad data collection needed during digital forensic investigations frequently clashes with meeting GDPR requirements because these investigations must exceed the minimum necessary data collection requirements. A multisector solution must be implemented to resolve these challenges by formulating comprehensive guidelines, international partnership programs, and privacy-enhancing technology deployment.

Operating processes that rely heavily on information have activated an increasing concern regarding the escalating cybercrime challenge. Law enforcement and investment institutions have established supportive policies that guide their investigation procedures and protocols. Current digital forensics standards without universal recognition led to increased difficulties when police agencies cooperate between different jurisdictions. The inability of two jurisdictions to accept evidence collected in one location prevents international criminal cases from proceeding successfully. The unique characteristics of digital evidence comprise its latent nature and its volatile and fragile properties while also its ability to shift across borders and depend on time and machine factors. Data integrity protection requires organizations to establish strong data governance frameworks while performing scheduled impact assessments and installing safe data transfer protocols when investigating cybercrimes.

Digital evidence can only be admitted in court when evidence management and chain of custody procedures are correctly executed. Modern cybercrime patterns demand that digital forensics teams create stable evidence-handling procedures. Due to its volatile nature, digital evidence presents difficulties for capture because investigators must conduct a detailed analysis followed by evidence extraction with proper preservation methods [56].

8.5 AI responsibility and legal limitations in forensic investigations

AI joins forensic investigations through better efficiency and accuracy while creating unprecedented difficulties in determining legal accountability limits during court procedures. Using AI-based methods in law enforcement digital forensics departments produces better case resolution results and expedited evidence analysis. AI machines and their machine learning capabilities are advanced digital examination instruments that swiftly analyse extensive data collections for analysts to obtain crucial evidence. The authorities operating within the criminal justice system implement AI technology because they recognise its benefits in using AI systems. The digital forensics industry keeps growing daily and requires advanced crime investigation methods. Forensic cyber investigation capabilities and security quality receive upgrades through human-AI system collaboration, as Points out. Full exploitation of AI technology in forensics requires solving problems related to technological aspects and legal and ethical issues caused by dishonest system applications across international fronts.

Adopting AI in forensic investigations generates extensive legal restrictions that require a comprehensive ethics analysis. The development process must achieve transparency by maintaining ethical standards that offer accountability to prevent discriminatory practices and ensure civil rights. There are insufficient resources to determine law enforcement readiness for using AI in cross-border investigations, which require strong criminal justice regulatory systems. Determining AI system abuse requires coordination between service providers and companies operating AI while police forces handle perpetrator identification. The involvement of AI systems in illicit conduct necessitates a thorough understanding of their functionalities and potential vulnerabilities. The legal boundaries set three rules to manage AI efficiency in forensic investigations through evidence admission, privacy measures, and algorithms bias management. Research-based investigations should thoroughly examine AI applications for sanction evaluation and justice program assessment because of their impact on risk taxation systems and evidence-based sanctioning operations. AI procedural criminal law needs specific legal standards because it utilises policing technology and cyber technology agents. There are significant reasons to doubt the reliability of information produced by AI systems during evidence assessment implementations [57]. The Los Angeles University College of Law continuously engages in active legal research about AI applications in criminal justice systems to protect fundamental legal principles and individual rights. Legal systems require full development to address specific issues AI causes throughout criminal procedures.

8.6 Ethical challenges in AI-driven IoMT forensics

Modern healthcare benefits from the AI and Internet of Medical Things merger by bringing distance patient care, diagnostic predictions, and individualised therapeutic approaches. IoMT innovation produces ethical complexities that affect IoMT forensic analysis, especially during investigations of medical device data for security needs, regulatory compliance and patient privacy protection. Multiple technical challenges emerge in forensic investigations targeting digital criminal evidence in IoMT systems because they use various medical devices that combine data structures with communication systems. IoMT devices transmit speed-sensitive patient information for medical care operations, but these systems become exposed to security threats, leading to improper medical choices and adverse healthcare effects. A systemised and ethical approach becomes vital for confirming IoMT evidence reliability to fulfil courtroom admission standards [58].

AI-driven IoMT forensic operations face their central ethical dilemma because they must determine how much investigation is moral while respecting patient data protection and privacy rights. The evaluation process for IoMT data and its analytical procedures expose protected healthcare records to possible unauthorised access. Protecting patient confidentiality requires medical investigation teams to respect HIPAA and GDPR privacy standards. Implementing AI algorithms in IoMT forensics creates complex ethical issues because these systems expose sensitive information and concurrently keep biased predictions that yield biased results. Systems that implement black box design in AI models hinder investigators from being able to locate where bias originates. Healthcare organisations need to build AI algorithms that carry high accuracy and transparency by implementing robust systems for performance auditing. Forensic investigations of Internet-connected medical technology have become complex because various operating systems and communication standards exist in devices. Investigators need specialised tools and particular expertise to proceed with the correct processing of this data. IoMT environment research and development requires continuous progress because ongoing medical device system updates and replacements necessitate constant forensic method advancement to stay efficient and valid.

Examination of IoMT data creates intricate legal and ethical complications when data transfer between nations occurs. Data sovereignty and jurisdictional regulations make it complicated for investigators to conduct their work. Essential healthcare facilities use intelligent medical devices that become targets for cybercriminals who want medical data and wish to disturb these critical systems. IoMT devices and their data protection require an organised protocol with multiple steps, starting from robust authentication with encryption and ending with intrusion detection systems. Standard forensic investigation procedures that work with IoMT devices establish essential needs to generate uniform investigation findings. Healthcare organisations must assign medical personnel to maintain patient accountability by implementing AI algorithms in their procedures. A clear definition of separate network boundaries is necessary since they provide critical functions when handling errors or facing biased conditions. Public networking has introduced complexities to the investigation process because of its shift from private systems.

This domain faces challenges beyond its technical requirements as it needs to construct preparedness mechanisms together with defining reporting systems and presentation rules and manage ethics-related problems, according to IoT device investigations face difficulty because of the vast spread of data. The forensic investigation receives artefact collections from individual IoT devices. The process of successfully investigating hidden vital device artefacts needs attention because public disclosure remains an unclear practice. Recent studies focus primarily on IoT security examinations, with privacy aspects as the central topic of investigation. The ethical challenges AI creates for IoMT forensic investigations need a wide range of collaboration between healthcare workers, forensic expert policymakers, and technology developers.

8.7 Conclusion

Healthcare is moving toward transformation at the intersection of AI and the Internet of Medical Things technology. This merges connected medical devices with remote patient observation and creates data-based improvements in clinical choices. IoMT ecosystems have expanded rapidly, which triggers advanced security concerns while creating fresh breaches through which unauthorised access to patient information co-occurs with medical device tampering that triggers healthcare service interruptions. IoMT forensics is an essential discipline for studying security incidents in IoMT infrastructure. IoMT forensics presents distinctive features because it interacts with multiple medical devices during streaming data operations under strict privacy and security regulations. New forensic investigation strategies based on AI technology are needed for complex IoMT incidents because conventional forensic methodologies demonstrate insufficient effectiveness. More interconnected devices create significant security threats, leading to progressively complex investigations. The technological need for an innovative approach to investigating IoT criminal activities requires urgent attention.

Multiple applications of AI-driven IoMT forensics will grow widely due to machine learning, deep learning, and natural language processing capabilities for enhancing forensic process stages. AI incident detection systems powered by AI conduct ongoing network investigations of IoMT devices to track data device activities and user actions for locating breaches and unauthorised movements. Security systems that absorb historical data alongside threat intelligence feed train themselves to identify security attacks and defend against threat vectors, therefore stopping security incidents from occurring. The development of thriving AI tools enables forensic investigators to gather and analyse evidence faster through their help in evidence management. The analysed IoMT datasets include medical records, device logs, and network traffic captures to find essential evidence for defining security incident sequences. The data sources include communication devices, sensor devices, automobiles, fridges, feed and drones.

Integrating AI technology, vulnerability assessment procedures, and risk management operations in IoMT environments achieves better results. Machine learning algorithms assess device configurations, network connections, and software versions for security weakness identification and priority remediation sequencing. Security stakeholders benefit from AI tools that generate modelling simulations to determine vulnerability impacts when identifying essential resources that need additional security defence. AI continuously monitors IoMT systems and networks to provide real-time system security information that lets teams plan suitable protective measures ahead of time. AI algorithms that analyse IoMT malware samples identify both committing devices, their propagation mechanics, and resultant patient functional impairments and safety risks. The systems using attacker procedure identification can predict future attacks before preventing them from happening. AI technology provides organisations with enhanced security policy management by recommending firewall rules, intrusion prevention systems, and other control modifications based on present threat advancements. An AI security solution transforms its defensive capabilities to suit different features within organisations’ digital environments. The expansion of data quantity or endpoint assets does not lead to additional costs or staffing requirements since AI and ML platforms independently scale up their surveillance capabilities. Organizations maintain functional security frameworks by allowing increased attack surface exposure as their business grows.

The duo of advanced analytical capability from AI with device complexity from IoMT devices has constructed a technical framework that causes traditional forensic procedures to fail. An effective AI governance system needs development to handle the specific operational needs within IoMT ecological systems. The established governance standards must create specific guidelines for data origin management and demonstrate algorithm procedures while defining responsibilities during equipment failures and exploitation situations.

Implementing AI governance within IoMT forensic investigations necessitates the creation of pre-established accountability paths that provide practical control standards. The definition of responsibility should first be given to AI developers, healthcare providers, and regulatory bodies to determine their accountability standards. A monitoring system with auditing functionality must be implemented to ensure that both technical operations match the intended functionality while upholding ethical principles and legal requirements. The governance system requires implementing procedures that aim to reduce the occurrence of AI algorithm bias. AI software accuracy relies only on training data because AI algorithms replicate all existing biases that the training information entails. The transparency of algorithms helps reduce biases because it enables the analysis of databases, algorithm frameworks, and decision-making systems. Systems need appropriate data selection processes and pre-processing to prevent biases, but operators should execute periodic checks to confirm that their operations maintain fairness and equity during all stages. AI system performance needs continuous evaluation to confirm functional operation and block destructive effects and unwanted results.
