Chapter 9AI-driven cyber forensics for critical healthcare infrastructure security

Sainag Nethala

DOI: 10.1201/9781003640325-9

9.1 Introduction

Modern infrastructure faces an increasing number of security threats due to growing system complexity and widespread digital adoption [1]. Organizations rely on interconnected systems to manage operations, making them prime targets for cyberattacks [2]. Threat actors exploit vulnerabilities in networks, software, and hardware to compromise security. Data breaches, financial losses, and operational disruptions result from these attacks [3]. Traditional security measures such as firewalls and antivirus software are insufficient against advanced threats. Attackers constantly develop new techniques, making real-time monitoring and rapid response essential [4]. Without an effective security framework, organizations struggle to prevent, detect, and respond to cyber threats. A proactive approach is necessary to confirm the safety and reliability of critical systems [5].

Forensic tools have become essential for analyzing security incidents and understanding attack patterns [6]. These tools help organizations investigate cyber threats by collecting and examining digital evidence. Forensic analysis identifies the source of attacks and provides insights for strengthening security. Early forensic methods focused on postincident investigations, which delayed responses and increased risks [7]. Modern forensic tools offer real-time detection, enabling security teams to take immediate action. Automated threat analysis enhances response times and minimizes damage [8]. Forensic technologies now incorporate advanced analytics to identify suspicious activities. These improvements make forensic tools a crucial component of cybersecurity strategies [9].

The evolution of forensic tools has significantly improved security measures, especially in medical IoT environments [10]. Healthcare systems integrate a wide range of connected devices, including medical sensors and patient monitoring systems. These devices collect and transmit sensitive health data, making security breaches a critical concern [11]. Traditional security solutions do not fully address the complexities of medical IoT environments. Attackers target healthcare infrastructure to exploit vulnerabilities in connected devices [12]. Unauthorized access to medical data can lead to severe privacy violations and operational failures. Forensic tools assist in monitoring medical IoT devices and detecting threats in real time [13]. A robust forensic framework is necessary to protect patient data and confirm system integrity.

Security information and event management (SIEM) platforms play a vital role in modern forensic analysis. These platforms aggregate security logs from multiple sources, providing centralized monitoring [14]. SIEM solutions help organizations detect anomalies and respond to threats quickly. Splunk is a leading SIEM platform that offers advanced data analytics for security operations [15]. It collects and processes large volumes of security logs to identify potential threats. Splunk enables security teams to correlate events and detect attack patterns in real time. The platform integrates with forensic tools to enhance investigative capabilities. By using SIEM solutions, organizations improve security visibility and response times [16].

Real-time forensic analysis has become essential for securing modern infrastructure against evolving threats. Traditional forensic methods relied on manual investigations, which were slow and inefficient [9, 17]. Cybercriminals now use advanced tactics to bypass security controls, making immediate threat detection necessary. Machine learning and artificial intelligence enhance forensic analysis by automating threat identification [18]. Behavioral analytics detect anomalies in system activities, indicating potential cyberattacks. Real-time monitoring reduces the time required to identify and respond to incidents [19]. Automated forensic tools provide instant insights into security threats. The integration of AI-driven forensic techniques improves overall cybersecurity effectiveness [20].

The medical industry faces unique security challenges due to the increasing use of IoT-based healthcare systems. Many medical devices are connected to hospital networks, creating new vulnerabilities [21]. Attackers exploit weaknesses in networked medical devices to access patient records [22]. Unauthorized modifications to medical equipment can lead to life-threatening situations. Healthcare organizations must implement strong forensic mechanisms to detect security breaches. Splunk provides real-time monitoring and log analysis for medical infrastructure. The platform helps hospitals track security events and respond to threats immediately [23]. Ensuring the security of medical IoT systems is crucial for protecting patient data and operational continuity.

Forensic tools play a crucial role in ensuring compliance with regulatory eF requirements. Many industries must adhere to security regulations to protect sensitive data [24]. In healthcare, frameworks such as HIPAA and HITECH mandate strict security controls [25]. Organizations that fail to comply with these regulations face legal and financial consequences [26]. Forensic tools assist in monitoring compliance by tracking security incidents. They provide detailed reports on security events, helping organizations meet regulatory requirements [27]. Splunk enhances compliance monitoring by generating automated reports and alerts. Integrating forensic tools with compliance frameworks strengthens data protection measures [16, 28].

Threat intelligence has become a fundamental aspect of forensic investigations. Organizations must stay ahead of cyber threats by analyzing attack trends [29]. Threat intelligence platforms collect data on known vulnerabilities and emerging attack patterns. Forensic tools integrate with these platforms to provide real-time threat insights [30]. By analyzing historical security incidents, organizations can predict future attack strategies [8]. Threat hunting techniques allow security teams to proactively search for indicators of compromise. Machine learning enhances threat intelligence by identifying hidden attack patterns. Forensic tools that use threat intelligence improve the accuracy of security investigations [31].

Incident response strategies depend on forensic analysis to make sure timely mitigation of security threats. A well-defined incident response framework enables organizations to contain cyberattacks effectively [32]. Forensic tools provide crucial insights into nature and impact of security breaches [33]. Real-time analysis helps security teams identify attack sources and prevent further damage [8, 34]. Automated forensic solutions accelerate incident response workflows by providing instant alerts. Organizations that implement strong forensic mechanisms can reduce the impact of cyber incidents [35]. Effective forensic analysis strengthens cybersecurity defenses against persistent threats. A proactive incident response approach minimizes operational disruptions [36].

The integration of forensic tools with SIEM platforms enhances security monitoring and threat detection [14, 37]. Splunk’s capabilities enable organizations to automate forensic investigations and improve security operations. Real-time forensic techniques reduce response times and increase situational awareness [38]. Machine learning-powered forensic analysis enhances the detection of advanced threats [39]. The combination of SIEM and forensic tools strengthens infrastructure security across industries [14]. Future advancements in forensic technologies will further improve cybersecurity resilience. Organizations must continuously evolve their forensic strategies to combat emerging cyber threats. A strong forensic framework confirms the long-term security and stability of critical infrastructure.

9.2 Literature review

The increasing complexity of modern digital infrastructure has led to a growing demand for robust forensic tools [40]. Traditional forensic methods relied on static analysis of system logs and network traffic, making real-time incident detection a challenge. Over time, digital forensic frameworks have evolved to incorporate machine learning (ML), deep learning, and real-time event correlation to improve security. For instance, the study [41] explores various forensic models used for detecting threats in IoT-based environments, highlighting the shift towards AI-driven analysis.

SIEM systems, particularly Splunk, play a crucial role in forensic analysis by aggregating and correlating security events from multiple sources. The study [42] examined how Splunk enhances log-based forensic investigations by detecting abnormal system behavior. Their analysis achieved an accuracy of 89.6% in detecting cyber threats. However, Splunk’s dependency on log availability and manual rule tuning presents limitations in adapting to sophisticated attacks.

Several research efforts have focused on ML-powered forensic analysis to enhance the detection and classification of cyber threats. Transfer learning-based intrusion detection models achieved an accuracy of 96.1% when applied to CSE-CIC-IDS2018 and IoT-23 datasets [43]. Similarly, the study [44] applied federated learning with CNN and LSTM, obtaining an accuracy of 95.3%. These findings suggest that deep learning models significantly improve forensic analysis by detecting complex attack patterns.

The increasing reliance on connected medical devices introduces significant security risks [45]. Network forensics frameworks have been proposed to secure Medical IoT (MIoT) systems. The study [46] focused on lightweight ML-based forensic techniques, achieving an accuracy of 90.5% in detecting anomalous network traffic in medical environments. However, challenges such as resource constraints and limited dataset diversity hinder the scalability of such approaches.

Recent forensic tools utilize hybrid models and ensemble learning techniques to enhance threat classification accuracy. The study [47] compared multiple forensic frameworks, achieving an accuracy of 91.8% using Decision Trees and anomaly detection techniques. Likewise, the research [48] proposed an ensemble ML approach for forensic analysis, demonstrating an accuracy of 94.5% on the IoT-23 and CIC-IDS2017 datasets. While these methods improve forensic capabilities, computational costs and overfitting risks remain key concerns.

Despite advancements in forensic methodologies, real-time threat detection remains a challenge. The study [49] highlighted the high false positive rates associated with anomaly detection systems, with a reported accuracy of 93.2%. Moreover, forensic models struggle to generalize across heterogeneous IoT networks, requiring frequent updates to maintain effectiveness.

Forensic frameworks must balance scalability, automation, and accuracy to handle large-scale cyber threats efficiently. Research on graph-based forensic models explored in [48] emphasized the importance of graph-based analytics for improving forensic accuracy. However, the computational complexity of such approaches makes them less suitable for real-time deployment. The paper [50] similarly found that forensic models must continuously evolve to counter new attack techniques, as static models become obsolete over time.

To enhance forensic efficiency, AI-driven automation, zero-trust frameworks, and predictive forensics are gaining traction. The study [43] proposed supervised learning based approaches with an accuracy of 96.1%, demonstrating the potential of machine learning in predictive forensic models. Future research should focus on adaptive security mechanisms that integrate real-time forensic analytics, SIEM automation, and AI-based cyber threat intelligence.

Despite advancements in forensic tools, real-time infrastructure protection still faces significant challenges. Many forensic frameworks depend on postincident analysis, which causes delays in detecting and responding to threats. This reactive approach limits their ability to prevent security breaches before they escalate. Scalability is another major issue, as forensic models struggle to handle large volumes of network traffic efficiently. When network activity increases, these systems often fail to process data in real time, making them ineffective for high-speed cyberattacks. Additionally, resource limitations in environments such as medical IoT systems create further obstacles. Many deep learning-based forensic tools require high computational power, which is difficult to provide in resource-constrained settings. This restricts the practical deployment of advanced AI-driven forensic solutions in critical infrastructure.

Some hybrid forensic models attempt to integrate artificial intelligence to improve detection capabilities, but they require substantial computing resources. This makes large-scale implementation challenging, especially in environments where computational efficiency is a priority. SIEM-based forensic tools offer some improvements in log analysis and security event correlation. However, they rely heavily on manual tuning, which makes it difficult to manage in fast-changing attack scenarios. Another problem is the high number of false positives generated by these systems. Security teams must spend significant time filtering through alerts, which slows down incident response. This reduces the overall effectiveness of forensic tools in protecting real-time infrastructure.

A key gap in forensic security is the weak integration between machine learning-based tools and SIEM systems. Most machine learning models struggle when applied to new environments because they are trained on limited datasets. This increases the risk of overfitting, making them unreliable for handling different types of cyber threats. Many existing forensic models are also ineffective against zero-day attacks. They rely on predefined patterns to detect threats, which means they fail to recognize entirely new attack strategies. Some advanced frameworks, such as those using federated learning and CNN-LSTM models, show potential for improving forensic capabilities. However, they require frequent retraining and continuous updates, making real-time deployment difficult. These challenges highlight the need for a robust forensic system that integrates machine learning, SIEM automation, and anomaly detection. Such a system must be scalable and efficient while minimizing the need for extensive computational resources (Table 9.1).

Table 9.1 Performance summary of forensic tools and technologies for securing infrastructure⏎
Ref. Dataset Method Limitations Acc Perc Rec F1
[41] UNSW- NB15, Bot-IoT Comparative forensic model study, XGBoost No real-time forensic automation 92.6 91.2 89.8 90.4
[42] CIC- IDS2017 Splunk-based log analysis, machine learning classifiers Relies on log availability, manual tuning required 89.6 87.3 85.9 86.5

9.3 Methodology

This section presents the proposed forensic framework for securing healthcare infrastructure, emphasizing real-time threat detection, Splunk-driven log analysis, and machine learning-enhanced forensic analytics. The methodology integrates SIEM automation, anomaly detection, and adaptive learning models to enhance cybersecurity defenses. Our framework is designed to collect, process, analyze, and respond to security incidents, minimizing false positives and optimizing forensic investigation as shown in Figure 9.1.

9.3.1 Problem formulation

9.3.1.1 Threat model and dataset overview

We define the healthcare infrastructure security problem using the IoT healthcare security dataset [51]. This dataset contains labeled logs representing both benign and malicious activities within medical IoT environments. Let X be the collection of log entries extracted from these datasets over time, where each log entry xi contains multiple features:

X = {x1,x2,…,xn}, xi ∈ Rd (9.1)

Each log entry consists of:

  • Temporal features: Timestamp Ti
  • Medical IoT attributes: device ID, data transmission frequency, sensor type
  • Security indicators: unauthorized access attempts, anomaly severity score

Given a labeled dataset
D = {{xi,  yi}}i = 1N
, the objective is to classify each log entry as either benign (y = 0) or malicious (y = 1), while detecting anomalies (y = −1) using an unsupervised model.

The classification function is defined as

$y_{i} = ~\left\{ \begin{matrix} {0,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~if~x_{i}~is~benign} \\ {1,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~if~x_{i}~is~malicious} \\ {- 1~,~~~~~~~~~~~~~if~x_{i}~is~anomalous} \\ \end{matrix} \right.$ (9.2)

where f maps an event xi to a security decision. The forensic system enhances Splunk SIEM capabilities by integrating machine learning-based anomaly detection and threat classification.

9.3.2 SIEM-driven log processing (Splunk)

9.3.2.1 Feature extraction from logs

Each event xi consists of structured and unstructured fields extracted from Splunk logs and the IoT healthcare security dataset. The extracted feature set is

X′ = {Ti,Devicei,Pi,Si,Ai,Li} (9.3)

where Ti = timestamp features, Pi = protocol type, Si = device communication entropy, Ai = attack classification, Li = log message embeddings (from Splunk search queries)

Splunk queries filter security logs and extract high-risk event patterns using:

S(xi) = log (F(xi)+1) + β1A(xi) + β2R(xi) (9.4)

where F(xi) = Log frequency score, A(xi) = anomaly detection probability, and R(xi) = severity score from historical Splunk logs

9.3.3 Graph-based event correlation

9.3.3.1 Graph construction from logs

To model relationships between log events, we construct a directed graph G where

G = (V,E) (9.5)

where nodes V represents medical IoT log events and edges (vi,vj) indicate event correlation based on shared anomaly signatures

The edge weight function for event correlation is

$w\left( {v_{i},v_{j}} \right) = \frac{\sum_{k}\delta\left( {x_{i},x_{j}} \right) \cdot A\left( x_{j} \right)}{\left| X \right|}$ (9.6)

where δ(xi,xj) measures log similarity and A(xj) represents the anomaly probability.

9.3.4 Anomaly detection using machine learning

9.3.4.1 Autoencoder-based unsupervised anomaly detection

For zero-day attack detection, we use an Autoencoder that learns normal log patterns and detects deviations. The encoder function is

h = σ(WeX′+ be) (9.7)

where We,be are learnable parameters. The decoder reconstructs

{X′}= σ(Wdh + bd) (9.8)

An anomaly score is computed as

$A\left( X^{\prime} \right) = \frac{\left| \left| {X^{\prime} - \hat{X^{\prime}}} \right| \right|^{2}}{\left| \left| X^{\prime} \right| \right|}$ (9.9)

High reconstruction error signals an anomalous event.

9.3.4.2 Supervised classification for attack detection

Using the IoT Healthcare Security Dataset, we train a CNN-LSTM hybrid model to classify threats. The model takes as input the transformed log vector X′ and predicts the attack type:

y = softmax(WcX′+bc) (9.10)

where Wc,bc are classification layer parameters.

9.3.5 Performance Evaluation

The forensic framework is evaluated using: $\text{Accuracy} = \frac{\text{TP} + \text{TN}}{\text{TP} + \text{TN} + \text{FP} + \text{FN}}$ (9.11)

$\text{F1-Score} = 2 \times \frac{\text{Precision} \times \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$ (9.12)

This methodology establishes a structured representation of the forensic framework, integrating SIEM-based threat detection, graph-based correlation, and deep learning techniques for securing medical IoT infrastructure.

9.4 Experiment settings

The forensic framework was implemented and evaluated on Google Colab Pro, utilizing high-performance GPUs (NVIDIA Tesla T4) to accelerate training and inference. The IoT Healthcare Security Dataset [51] was preprocessed and stored in Google Drive, ensuring seamless data access. All machine learning models, anomaly detection algorithms, and Splunk-based log analysis were executed in a Python 3.10 environment with TensorFlow, PyTorch, Scikit-learn, and NetworkX installed. Splunk Cloud was configured to receive and process security logs, enabling real-time event monitoring and correlation.

The dataset preprocessing involved handling missing values, normalizing numerical attributes, and encoding categorical variables. Medical IoT-related features such as device ID entropy, transmission frequency, sensor type, and timestamp correlations were extracted to enhance forensic detection capabilities. Feature selection was performed using principal component analysis and recursive feature elimination to reduce dimensionality while maintaining critical forensic indicators. Graph-based event correlation was conducted using NetworkX, creating an attack signature network where nodes represented log events and edges defined anomaly correlations.

For the machine learning-based attack detection, an Autoencoder model was trained to detect zero-day anomalies, while a CNN-LSTM hybrid classifier was developed for multiclass attack classification. The models were trained on 80% of the dataset and validated on 20%, ensuring a balanced evaluation. Adam optimizer with a learning rate of 0.0001 was used, and models were trained for 50 epochs with batch size 512. Performance metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and ROC-AUC were recorded for each experiment. The models were deployed in a real-time log stream from Splunk to test their ability to detect live threats.

To validate the effectiveness of real-time forensic analysis, Splunk SIEM alerts were triggered based on anomaly scores and attack severity levels. A threshold-based incident response was implemented where threats exceeding a predefined risk score were automatically flagged for quarantine or IP blocking. Latency measurements were recorded to evaluate threat detection speed, comparing machine learning-based approaches vs. traditional rule-based Splunk alerts. The results demonstrated a significant improvement in response time and detection accuracy, validating the feasibility of integrating AI-driven forensic methodologies within SIEM systems.

9.5 Results and analysis

The evaluation of the proposed forensic framework was conducted using the IoT healthcare security dataset. This dataset provides diverse medical IoT traffic logs containing both benign and malicious activities, allowing for a thorough assessment of the forensic system’s performance in a healthcare environment. The evaluation was carried out in terms of accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and detection latency, which are critical indicators of an intrusion detection system’s efficiency in detecting threats within medical infrastructure.

9.5.1 Benchmarking against state-of-the-art forensic models

To validate the performance of the proposed forensic system, its results were benchmarked against six existing models from literature, including IoT-forensic frameworks, machine learning-based detection models, and SIEM-driven anomaly detection systems. The comparison is presented in Table 9.2.

Table 9.2 Performance comparison of forensic detection models⏎
Method Acc Prec Rec F1 Latency
Forensic XGBoost model [41] 92.6 91.2 89.8 90.4 112
Federated learning model [44] 95.3 94.1 92.8 93.4 105
IoT-forensics framework [47] 91.8 90.5 89.3 89.9 120
Anomaly detection in IDS [48] 94.5 93.3 92.0 92.6 101
Hybrid ML model [49] 93.2 92.0 90.7 91.3 98
Proposed model 97.6 96.8 95.9 96.3 72

The results clearly indicate that the proposed forensic model outperforms previous approaches across all evaluation metrics. By integrating Splunk-based log analysis, machine learning-driven anomaly detection, and event correlation techniques, the framework significantly improves forensic accuracy, reduces false positives, and accelerates incident response times.

9.5.2 Analysis of accuracy and F1-score

The most crucial metric in intrusion detection is accuracy, as it determines the proportion of correctly classified events. Additionally, F1-score provides a balanced evaluation by considering both precision and recall. Figures 9.2 and 9.3 presented comparisons of these metrics. The proposed forensic system achieved an accuracy of 97.6%, which is 4–6% higher than traditional forensic models. The enhanced F1-score of 96.3% further confirms the reliability of the classification system, minimizing the trade-off between precision and recall. Superior performance stems from log-based feature engineering and adaptive event correlation, which allows the model to differentiate between benign and malicious events more effectively.

9.5.3 Impact of precision and recall on threat detection

Precision represents the proportion of correctly identified attack instances, while recall measures the system’s ability to detect all malicious activities. An effective forensic model must maintain a high precision-recall balance to avoid both false positives and missed threats. Figures 9.4 and 9.5 illustrate the comparison.

The proposed model recorded a precision of 96.8%, reducing false alarms and unnecessary forensic investigations. Similarly, the recall value of 95.9% shows that a wide range of attack types is accurately detected. In contrast, traditional rule-based forensic models exhibit lower recall due to rigid alert mechanisms that fail to adapt to evolving attack patterns.

9.5.4 Minimizing detection latency for real-time response

One of the key challenges in forensic security is reducing detection latency. Traditional SIEM solutions often suffer from delayed response times due to inefficient rule processing and manual log filtering. Figure 9.6 demonstrates the latency reduction achieved by the proposed forensic framework.

By integrating AI-driven log filtering and graph-based event correlation, the proposed framework reduces detection latency to 72 ms, a 40% improvement over previous approaches. Faster detection enables real-time security enforcement, allowing organizations to respond to cyber threats before they escalate.

9.5.5 Comprehensive overview of model performance

To visualize the overall improvements achieved by the proposed framework, a group bar chart comparing all metrics is shown in Figure 9.7.

The grouped comparison clearly highlights that the proposed model consistently outperforms all baseline methods. The integration of Splunk-driven forensic intelligence, AI-powered anomaly detection, and graph-based event correlation vizierial superior accuracy, faster detection, and reduced false positive rates.

9.5.6 Effectiveness of graph-based event correlation

Traditional forensic systems analyze logs in isolation, missing relationships between security events. The proposed framework constructs attack signature graphs that link correlated events, significantly improving forensic intelligence. Figure 9.8 illustrates this approach. Each node in the attack graph represents a security event, while edges signify correlations based on log similarity and anomaly signatures. This enables the forensic system to uncover hidden attack patterns that traditional methods fail to detect.

9.5.7 Experimental findings

The experimental evaluation of the forensic framework confirms its superiority in securing critical healthcare infrastructure. The model demonstrates significant advancements in multiple aspects of forensic security. It achieves higher accuracy and detection precision by utilizing adaptive AI-driven log filtering, which improves classification reliability and ensures a more precise identification of threats in medical IoT environments. Additionally, the proposed system significantly reduces response times by optimizing real-time event processing, enabling immediate security enforcement and minimizing the delay in detecting potential cyber threats targeting healthcare systems. The framework also lowers false positives through graph-based event correlation, which enhances forensic intelligence by eliminating redundant alerts and reducing unnecessary investigations related to unauthorized access and abnormal device behavior. Moreover, the system is highly scalable for large-scale hospital networks due to its efficient real-time log processing, allowing healthcare organizations to handle high-volume forensic data without performance degradation. These findings validate that the integration of SIEM, AI-driven anomaly detection, and graph-based correlation strengthens forensic security, ensuring a more robust and efficient approach to medical infrastructure protection. These results validate that forensic automation using SIEM and AI-driven anomaly detection bridges the gap between traditional healthcare security monitoring and real-time forensic intelligence.

9.6 Conclusion

The proposed forensic framework provides a robust and scalable solution for securing critical healthcare infrastructure by integrating Splunk-based log analysis, AI-driven anomaly detection, and graph-based event correlation. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over traditional forensic models, particularly in accuracy, precision, recall, and response time. The model effectively reduces false positives while maintaining a high detection rate, ensuring a reliable classification of both known and unknown threats. The use of adaptive machine learning techniques enhances the identification of threats, allowing the system to evolve with emerging attack patterns in medical IoT environments. Furthermore, the reduction in detection latency to 72 ms highlights the efficiency of real-time log processing, making it feasible for large-scale hospital and healthcare network security applications. Using automated forensic intelligence, medical organizations can detect and mitigate threats proactively, preventing cyberattacks before they compromise patient data or disrupt healthcare operations. The graph-based event correlation further strengthens forensic analysis by identifying hidden attack patterns, ensuring that security teams have a comprehensive view of attack propagation across interconnected medical devices. The proposed approach demonstrates how combining SIEM with machine learning and real-time event analysis leads to a more efficient, intelligent, and automated forensic security solution tailored for healthcare infrastructure.

Despite these advancements, there are still areas that require further exploration and refinement. The reliance on historical log data means that the system’s performance is influenced by data availability and quality. While the model successfully detects known and novel attacks, improving its ability to adapt to zero-day threats without relying on prior data remains an open challenge. Additionally, optimizing computational efficiency for real-time threat detection in resource-constrained environments such as medical IoT networks and embedded healthcare devices is crucial for broader applicability. Future work can focus on enhancing the automation of forensic response mechanisms, enabling the system to autonomously mitigate threats without human intervention. Moreover, the integration of blockchain-based forensic logging can improve data integrity and traceability, making forensic records tamper-proof and enhancing compliance with healthcare regulations such as HIPAA. The insights gained from this research establish a strong foundation for the continued development of intelligent forensic security frameworks that ensure resilient, adaptive, and highly responsive threat detection systems for securing modern healthcare infrastructure.

Chapter 10Sustainable forensicsEnergy efficiency and resource management for the future

P. Sudheer, M. Saravanan, Immanuel Anupalli, and S. Ashmad

DOI: 10.1201/9781003640325-10

10.1 Introduction

At its very beginning is eco-friendly forensics, which is a little into the field of integrating green processes into forensic science. However, since high-energy laboratory tests, digital evaluations, and nonbiodegradable materials are used on a regular basis in forensic investigations, especially, the need for sustainable practice that reduces environmental damage and moves to positive investigations seems important. Unfortunately, however, conventional forensic science sources typically require large amounts of energy, highly dangerous chemicals, and large amounts of data management leaving a large carbon footprint. However, eco-friendly forensics will tackle these challenges by establishing energy savings laboratories, use of renewable energy sources, eco-friendly operations in crime and use of the latest digital intelligence skills like artificial intelligence, Internet of Things (IoT), and cloud technology. Forensic labs use huge power while machines like the GC-MS machines, DNA analyzers, electron microscopes and the like consume a lot of power. Laboratory buildings are additionally dependent on high-energy heating ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) systems for stable conditions during forensic valuations. Just as the forensic science industry also produces large quantities of waste of chemicals that contain toxins, biological samples, as well as the single-use plastics used in crime scene work, the industry that includes energy use does as well. As a result, with the use of nonbiodegradable items such as gloves, swabs, and packaging materials which most of the time are thrown into landfills, leads to environmental damage. However, this depletion of resources is the reason why the change in forensics methods has to migrate to digital and paperless because paper documentation methods are part as well. The sweet spot is renewable energy in labs—as the best way to shave the carbon footprint in forensic science. Now, solar panel, wind turbine, and battery storage have become increasingly crucial to forensic facilities as they are no longer as heavily reliant on fossil fuels. Energy intelligent insulated structures and automated smart forensics labs with artificial intelligence (AI)-based energy efficient management system which works to curb energy consumption but at the same point it also improves the energy infrastructure efficiency by itself. By changing the structure, many IoT applications turn forensic equipment to efficient monitoring so devices can only work as required to save energy and to prolong the time of expensive forensic tool [1]. Because AI is used in predictive maintenance, the use of AI in predictive maintenance is really helpful as it helps predict the failure of the equipment very much before it actually does, otherwise load loss and downtimes in forensic processes are significantly reduced. Digital forensics is another rapidly developing field that increases the possibility of forensics science in support of sustainability. Low power costs involve reduced energy reduction and reduced electronic waste because of the formation of on-site data centers in order to reduce excess energy. These make the efficiency increase, as the data are better handled, and the forensic evidence is also managed in a secure and tamper-proof way. In addition, plans have been made for programs in the recycling of electronic waste as a solution to environmental impact.

10.2 Sustainable infrastructure and energy efficiency in forensic laboratories

10.2.1 Environmentally friendly design of forensic labs utilizing renewable energy sources

Forensic laboratories are created to be more eco-friendly and have zero energy consumption, no waste, and include innovative technologically modern methods of being environmentally friendly. A green forensic lab must use renewable energy, energy recycling devices, and sustainable building resources, and advanced energy monitoring systems. These features can substantially reduce energy use in forensic labs without affecting their efficiency. Solar power is one of the renewable energy sources frequently being used in forensic science. Today, photovoltaic (PV) solar panels provide electricity to run the facility at many forensic labs. Advantages of having solar-powered forensic labs include a smaller carbon footprint from the use of fossil fuel, saving on electricity costs, and good backup power source to use during power outages for forensic work. Forensic facilities that have undergone transition to the use of solar energy laboratories are a number of these facilities and such transition is aimed at achieving energy sustainability, especially in regions that have plenty of sun. Another promising option for the wind energy is for the forensic science facilities. Wind turbines can generate a considerable amount of energy, both onshore and offshore, hence are suitable energies for use by forensic labs, somewhere where the wind speed is high. Forensic labs can experience a steady supply of electricity being provided by wind-powered labs, decreased reliance on traditional energy sources [2], and follow through with and comply with green energy regulations imposed in forensic labs. As the regulations on wind energy become more stringent in many countries, forensic research centers are now integrating wind power systems in order to boost their independence with respect to energy. One additional renewable resource available in physical forensic laboratories located near rivers, reservoirs or other water sources is hydroelectric energy. Small-scale hydro plant offers a steady and sustainable electricity supply that does not shift forensic activities. Organizations can notably reduce energy and carbon emissions while still providing a guaranteed power supply for their labs by partnering with hydroelectric power facilities.

10.2.2 Energy-saving HVAC systems, lighting, and smart grid technology for forensic labs

The precision of forensic analysis relies heavily on the proper regulation of temperature, humidity, and air quality in forensic labs. These laboratories consume a significant share of energy use in the operations costs with conventional HVAC system amounting to many amounts of energy. Apparently, besides significantly increasing the air quality, the IoT becomes realized in the air filtration system with saving a lot of the energy and reusing the waste heat to improve efficiency. These geothermal heating and cooling systems will also help to control laboratory temperatures effectively without much negative impact on the environment.

Lighting is highly responsible for the energy used in forensic laboratories. By choosing to switch to smart LED lights, they have made the switches work. Lighting systems that turn off when no movement is detected also conserve energy in that regard. Consequently, LED systems are able to sense the daylight and adjust their brightness automatically based on the natural light present and decrease the amount of energy waste. AI-powered intelligence light networks result in networks that manage how much or how less energy is consumed in turning light levels up and down depending on the lighting needs for forensic work. Currently, numerous of modern forensic labs’ have already adopted the AI-empowered and sensor-based lighting that enhances both the efficiency and working conditions of forensic experts.

Forensic labs have become more advanced in their daily operations and increasingly adopting new facilities such as smart grids and demand response operations, therefore, there is a need to create extensions of such new facilities for the successful completion of an entire case. But AI and the IoT are making these laboratories smart and energetically efficient [3]. These technologies then come out of the laboratory and are shown to forensic labs where they can view themselves, control their energy use, and enhance operational effectiveness. AI helps energy management systems to analyze expected energy consumption trends, identify inefficiencies and exploit energy conservation chance. To save energy, automated operations in the labs can turn off these unused devices, as well as preventive maintenance tools will find potential failures in forensic equipment before it happens.

They use IoT smart sensors by forensic labs to periodically and directly inform energy usage. They are automatically able to control lighting, HVAC systems, and laboratory equipment in such a manner as to not allow the use of the energy to harm the quality of forensic analysis. In addition, energy-saving actions anticipated by IoT that are based on the data, also depend on the data. It is the most successful of any forensic institution’s sustainability practices in the industry. For astronomically reducing the bills of energy, more and more forensic lab are adopting IoT automation for better standards in crime investigations.

Many forensic laboratories worldwide have adopted the trend towards net zero energy, and the forensic laboratories are proving that forensic science no longer needs to occur in a nonsustainable way and even lower its performance. There is a case here in the Netherlands Forensic Institute (NFI) that is incorporating solar energy, AI, driven energy management as well as smart heating and cooling systems to make the whole structure energy efficient.

Through the use of automation, NFI has cut down energy use by 40% while still excelling in forensic investigations. In the United States, many forensic labs have obtained the Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design (LEED) certification, which promotes the use of renewable energy, environmentally friendly building materials, and strategies for minimizing waste [4]. These initiatives have led to a 30% decrease in carbon output and an enhancement in the efficiency of forensic operations. A similar forensic research center in Germany has reached net-zero energy consumption by utilizing smart grids, AI-driven lab management, and IoT-based forensic oversight. This lab serves as an example for the adoption of sustainable forensic technologies in law enforcement agencies globally.

10.3 Green practices in forensic science and laboratory operations

10.3.1 Introduction

Forensic science has always been vital in solving crimes; however, traditional forensic methods often lead to significant environmental harm due to the use of hazardous substances, materials that don’t break down, and energy-intensive lab procedures. A new focus on sustainable resource management within forensic science aims to lessen the environmental impact of forensic work while maintaining high standards for precision and dependability. Some strategies that forensic labs can employ to shift towards environmentally friendly methods include applying green chemistry concepts, decreasing toxic waste, choosing eco-friendly materials, and adopting improved procurement practices. This chapter will explore the adoption of green chemistry, approaches to waste reduction, environmentally conscious sample treatment, and the use of biodegradable or recyclable materials within sustainable resource management in forensic science [5]. The chapter will also highlight the procurement processes of forensic institutions, ensuring that tools and chemicals are sourced from responsible and ethical suppliers.

10.3.2 Adoption of green chemistry in forensic analysis

It is considered the basic architectural aspect of sustainable forensic science to conduct green chemistry by decreasing the application of harmful substances and eliminating chemical waste. Many toxic solvents, reagents, and methods used in traditional forensic techniques can cause environmental damage and are potentially unsafe practices for forensic workers. Principles of green chemistry in forensic science are promoted with the trend towards safer alternatives that do not contain toxicity, environmentally friendly solvents, and reagents, which are biodegradable. Advancements in green chemistry have especially contributed to forensic toxicology and drug testing. One of the common trends is that researchers have developed microscale analytical methods that require significantly less reagent yet achieve the same level of precision. In addition, as a source of crime scene investigation testing has replaced the chemically intensive forensic testing techniques of old—first used in the early 20th century—water-based extraction and enzymatic reactions have replaced more damaging bio-based solvents, all to reduce the environmental impact of forensic analysis. If forensic labs can adopt green chemistry, safety, sustainability and operational efficiency in their day-to-day operations will not be hindered by analytical precision [6].

10.3.3 Reducing hazardous waste in forensic laboratories

Forensic labs generate a variety of hazardous waste products, including chemical leftovers, biological specimens, expired chemicals, and equipment that has been contaminated. If these materials are not discarded appropriately, it could harm the environment and pose risks to human health. Therefore, effective waste management practices are essential for reducing the waste created in forensic labs and adhering to environmental safety guidelines. One effective technique for minimizing waste is the use of microfluidic and miniaturized methods for forensic analysis, which significantly lower the number of reagents needed and help prevent the creation of chemical waste. Forensic laboratories can utilize chemical recycling by recovering unused portions of chemicals from secure containers and reusing them instead of throwing them away. Additionally, solvent recovery systems clean and recycle solvents for repeated use, leading to less hazardous waste overall. Biological waste, such as DNA and blood samples, requires careful disposal to protect the environment. Methods such as incineration, biodegradation, and enzymatic breakdown are used to ensure that biological materials are disposed of safely without causing harmful pollution. Implementing strict guidelines for separating waste, improving disposal procedures, and finding eco-friendly packaging methods enhances the sustainability of forensic labs while protecting the environment.

10.3.4 Eco-friendly sampling collection, storage, and analysis

It should be the process of gathering, keeping, and examining forensic evidence which minimizes the impact on environment that it has to waste and energy consumption. Single-use plastics, chemically treated swabs, and overly packaged solutions are commonly used by many forensic investigations and all of these items contribute to environmental harm. The collection and storage of the evidence is done using alternative materials such as biodegradable, reusable, and recyclable materials. For example, taking the example of biological samples, forensic professionals now choose for plant-based biopolymers and compostable packaging on top of plastic storage of such samples. Reusing evidence containers that are made of glass or a composable waste product also helps eliminate plastic waste from the equation. In addition to that, forensic labs are moving towards more energy-efficient storage options such as smart energy-saving refrigerators or smart climate-controlled lockers, that will diminish electricity usage and will assure the integrity of the evidence. This also applies to fingerprint analysis and crime scene investigation. Traditional fingerprinting methods usually involve chemical treatments that lead to the production of hazardous byproducts. However, newer and greener methods employ water-based, nontoxic fingerprint powders and the nontoxic lifting methods using infrared imaging and biodegradable material for coming up with powder lift fingerprints [7].

10.3.5 Recyclable and recyclable materials used in medical research

The amount of waste generated in medical practices varies, especially from single-use plastics, paper products, and disposable glasses. Focusing on recyclable and recyclable materials is essential to improve the sustainability of research. Many organizations have started using plant-based cotton swabs, compostable gloves, and environmentally friendly protective equipment to reduce their reliance on plastic.

The use of recycled packaging in research work is increasing. Many laboratories have chosen paper bags, biodegradable seals, and reusable labels as evidence. Instead of plastic crime-fighting tape, investigative agencies are now exploring ways to destroy them from corn. In addition, forensic laboratories have implemented digital data management systems that reduce paper usage and promote paperless investigation processes.

Advances in forensic security are further supported by the use of recyclable glass, recyclable evidence, and energy-efficient technologies by prioritizing recyclable and reusable materials, investigative agencies can reduce waste.

10.3.6 Eco-friendly purchasing of forensic tools and chemicals

Sustainability in forensic science goes beyond what happens in the lab; it also involves how forensic tools, chemicals, and equipment are acquired. Choosing materials that are friendly to the environment helps ensure that forensic work is both precise and ethical. Many forensic organizations are now adopting green purchasing policies that focus on suppliers who have eco-certifications, sustainable manufacturing practices, and eco-friendly packaging. Various forensic instruments, such as DNA extraction kits, chromatography columns, and tools for crime scene analysis, are increasingly made from recyclable and nontoxic materials. Some forensic labs are advancing towards digital methods and cloud analysis, which reduces reliance on physical tools, thus cutting down on resource use. Additionally, forensic agencies are teaming up with manufacturers that prioritize sustainable practices, ensuring that the chemicals and reagents used in investigations comply with environmental safety regulations [8]. An effective strategy in procurement is to apply circular economy principles, where tools and equipment are made to last, be repairable, or recyclable. Instead of discarding old forensic tools, labs are opting for refurbishment and repurposing initiatives to increase the longevity of tools and decrease electronic waste. A sustainable procurement policy also promotes the use of biodegradable swabs, lab coats made from recyclable materials, and nontoxic dyes in forensic testing, while aligning operations with global environmental objectives.

10.3.7 Use of biodegradable and recyclable materials in forensic investigations

Plastics, paper records, and disposable protective personnel are some of the waste that comes from all forensic investigations and the amount is especially huge if they are single-use plastics. On the other hand, the investigations will be more sustainable if biodegradable recyclable materials are adopted. Today, many organizations have embraced using plant-based swabs, compostable gloves, and eco-friendly protective clothing, among other synthetic alternatives, as an alternative to using synthetic items. In addition, there is a growing trend to use recyclable packaging in the forensic context. Many labs are choosing biodegradable sealants, paper evidence bags, and reusable markers for evidence and some even prefer disposable plastic seals, facial tissue, gloves, etc. But plastic crime scene tape has already reached its limits, so the days of making corn starch polymers have passed and forensic agencies have started using biodegradable markers. At present, the emphasis is on paperless detection and usage of electronic documentation systems so the use of papers in forensic labs is on a decreasing trend. Sustainability is also performed in the field of forensic analysis by means of refillable ink pads and other energy-efficient forensic imaging methods, in conjunction with recyclable glass slides. If biodegradable and recyclable materials are taken into consideration, then the whole of the waste which is sent to landfills can be greatly reduced in forensic institutions.

10.4 Sustainable digital forensics and green IT solutions

10.4.1 Overview of digital forensics and eco-friendly IT practices

Digital forensics plays a crucial role in today’s law enforcement. It includes gathering, protecting, analyzing, and presenting digital proof. Nevertheless, the growing reliance on digital forensic work has led to high energy use, significant carbon emissions, and rising electronic waste. Forensic labs and data centers typically need substantial computing power, with servers operating around the clock to manage large amounts of forensic data. Moreover, the cooling systems in these centers use a significant portion of energy. Thus, implementing green IT in digital forensics is necessary to promote sustainability while ensuring that investigations remain accurate. Sustainable practices in digital forensics involve using energy-efficient servers, cloud services, and edge computing, which help improve resource distribution while reducing energy usage [9]. The adoption of AI-powered forensic automation helps decrease unnecessary energy consumption by smartly prioritizing investigative tasks. Furthermore, blockchain technology is transforming digital evidence management by providing decentralized and tamper-resistant forensic records, avoiding redundant data storage and high-energy verification processes. By incorporating these green IT approaches, digital forensics can progress towards more environmentally friendly operations, which will sustain its importance in contemporary criminal investigations [10].

10.4.2 Energy consumption of data centers and forensic labs

One of the largest users of energy in digital forensic labs is data centers, due to the high energy needs of advanced computing, storage, and analytical tools for extensive operations. Processes such as disk imaging, cryptanalysis, and malware analysis require numerous operational servers, leading to significant energy demands, which also necessitate powerful cooling systems to keep them at the right temperature; this, in turn, adds to energy consumption. A single top-performing forensic workstation can use as much as 600 watts per hour, while larger forensic data centers may need megawatts of electricity to function continuously. Additionally, redundancy in data storage and forensic case management results in excessive duplicated digital evidence, increasing the energy needed for both storage and retrieval [10]. This significant energy usage not only escalates operational expenses but also adds to carbon emissions and harm to the environment. Consequently, forensic organizations must explore eco-friendly computing methods that improve digital forensic processes while lessening energy consumption.

10.4.3 Energy-efficient servers, cloud computing, and edge computing

The best way to achieve more sustainability in digital forensics is to move towards energy-efficient computing setup. Low-energy servers, upgraded data centers, and forensic systems in the cloud can reduce the power usage with little sacrifice in operational effectiveness. Green data centers are all about conserving energy with technologies like highly efficient blade servers, coolants like chilled water, and AI-based resource management which does not waste energy. Today, solar and wind energy aid forensic labs in working to be more environmentally friendly. One of its advantages is AI-driven energy management which enables forensic processing tasks to be more efficient, and those resources to be only used when needed. With cloud computing becoming a hot topic, the digital forensic investigations have been changed, and the alternative to the traditional physical data center is an eco-friendly cloud computing. Forensic labs in the cloud make it easy for investigators to access the digital evidence from around the world without taking away from any physical space. It also appropriates the resource better in computing, so that forensic computing power is used right. Storage of forensic data in virtual spaces also minimizes the energy used for corresponding hardware. The benefit of edge computing in this case is that it takes advantage of processing forensic data closer to its source. The final result is that forensic data is not sent to central cloud servers for analysis, thereby lowering the bandwidth and server capacity strain on network. Instead, real-time investigations on the device level occur, with response times improved and power use dropped. Edge AI models that automate processes can be used by forensic professionals to conduct investigations without high energy-consuming computing resources [11].

The future of how investigations will be done utilizing the digital forensic investigation will use artificial. It can be used to automate or shorten evidence examination, aid in the identification of cyber threat, among others. AI forensic tools however require heavy computing power. Therefore, forensic scientists build energy-efficient AI models with enough processing power to sustain during an investigation, but they also do not compromise accuracy on it. In investigating cybercrime activities, AI is used to handle the activities to find a pattern of fraud as real time to reduce the normal forensic table view. Forensic computing body resources do not consume the major extents of energy when funny cyber threat identification is carried out through low-energy models based on AI. AI also creates a better method of storing forensic data, by eliminating the dupes through automatic categorization and smart case management. AI-based forensic automation requires low total computational energy, total computational energy consumption, as well as this eliminates total computational energy consumption, while reducing total computational energy consumption and healthy cybersecurity analysis [12].

Blockchain technology is becoming a reliable and energy-efficient option for managing forensic evidence. Forensic databases typically require substantial server power, backups, and centralized systems. They employ authentication methods that result in high energy consumption. Forensic techniques based on blockchain provide a decentralized digital record that captures forensic data in a tamper-proof manner, promoting reliability in forensic investigations while reducing the reliance on energy-intensive forensic case management systems. The application of blockchain in forensic science creates permanent case records, eliminating the necessity for storing duplicate information. Automated procedures using smart contracts improve forensic workflows, boosting energy efficiency by lowering the processing demands of standard forensic case handling. Additionally, zero-knowledge proof methods allow investigators to confirm digital evidence without needing to decrypt complete forensic datasets, which significantly cuts down processing needs and storage space. Implementing blockchain in forensic decentralization helps to lower energy usage, improve cybersecurity, and create more efficient processes for evidence verification. Numerous forensic agencies today are looking into how to implement eco-friendly blockchain strategies that utilize low-energy consensus algorithms to further promote sustainability in digital forensic research. The combination of AI and the IoT is transforming forensic science, especially by developing labs that are more sustainable, effective, and secure. These advancements not only improve investigation accuracy but also foster sustainability by enhancing resource usage. In forensic labs, sensors connected through IoT track important conditions such as temperature, humidity, light intensity, and air quality, all of which are essential for maintaining evidence integrity. AI analyzes this real-time information to improve energy efficiency and automate adjustments within laboratory settings. For instance, AI-based systems can regulate heating, ventilation, air conditioning, lighting, and equipment operation to reduce energy loss while still meeting the necessary standards for forensic analysis.

This has led to the importance of the enhancement of forensic laboratories to make them more advanced smart labs with energy management automation. These labs traditionally require a lot of energy, including for maintaining the proper environmental conditions as well as simply because complex scientific instruments make a lot of demands on power. The smart grids and automated systems can import energy on a real-time basis and monitor its consumption. The use of AI models to predict fluctuation in energy usage and optimize the operation schedule of high-energy devices such as imaging equipment or spectrometer to consume them during low-demand times or reduced power levels would reduce costs and impact the environment. Additionally, entry of AI for forecasted upkeep into forensic labs allows the ongoing monitoring of the equipment’s state by incorporating IoT sensors. They can detect such early signs of equipment failure in critical forensic devices, such as microscopes, chromatographs, or DNA analyzers. Laboratory technicians will get the notification on these sensors that the AI algorithms will evaluate the data of such sensors on when a device is likely to fail or need servicing, so they can do maintenance work proactively. A proactive strategy as opposed to being reactive means we can come to a more eco-friendly operation as we actually minimize unexpected downtime, extend the life of expensive equipment and keep unnecessary repairs from accumulating along the way. The IoT also helps in real time monitoring of the environment [13].

For instance, in a forensic laboratory, temperature or relative humidity fluctuations result in a decrease in evidence. Using advanced sensors that are on the IoT devices, current conditions can be constantly monitored and users are warned if there’s any significant difference from the baseline conditions. This will make forensic examinations conducted under the best possible conditions, saving evidence from degradation by legal and chemical activity. Finally, IoT gadgets can monitor energy use throughout the lab to provide a clear breakdown of the most energy-intensifying systems and areas for improvement in efficiency. Finally, 5G technology brings with it an improvement in forensic data analysis by offering quicker data transfers as well as instant processing. IoT sensors and forensic tools can generate immense volumes of data that will overwhelm centralized systems unless they are effectively managed. 5G networks allow for very low data delays, allowing for a faster decision making. Edge computing is any computer center where data is processed on the spot rather than being sent to a central server. It goes to speed with analyses and responses which is critical in forensic work because the sooner the evidence is looked at, the more likely the investigation goes in one direction or other. With the deployment of edge computing and AI at the forensic labs, data processing and critical analyses become faster, and hence, it enhances on both efficiency and accurate results of forensic tasks. In addition, the integration of AI, IoT, 5G, and edge computing serves as an umbrella term for a broader modernization that contributes, through sustainability, efficiency, and an imminent ability to handle the increasing needs of the forensic sector [14]. These technologies not only improve operational effectiveness, but they also help maintain the integrity through the process in order to yield higher accuracy and reliability in investigations. Furthermore, combining blockchain and forensic science with circular economy principles leads to the innovation and sustainability through full transparency, reducing waste, as well as improving the overall authenticity of the forensic practice.

10.4.4 Blockchain and circular economy in forensics

A major advancement in blockchain-powered evidence tracking is improved transparency and security in forensic investigations. The decentralized and unchangeable ledger system of blockchain serves as an excellent solution for managing the chain of custody for evidence. Every step taken regarding an item, like collection, transport, analysis, or storage, can be logged on the blockchain, ensuring all actions are securely documented in a way that cannot be tampered with. This system provides unmatched transparency: altering or manipulating the evidence without detection is nearly impossible. Furthermore, blockchain allows involved parties to track the complete history of the evidence in real time, making it easier to hold accountable all associated actions.

More responsible investigations which are thorough, and free of mistakes. By adopting blockchain technology, it minimizes the need for paper-based records and physical records which leads to carbon emissions reduction as well as a reduction of administrative cost from an eco-friendly point of view. At the same time, the methods of circular economy are increasingly being used in the field of forensic science, especially in the management of forensic waste and resources. Forensic laboratory’s waste is very much and includes chemical leftovers and disposable materials used in the evidence examination. Circular economy is mostly centered around the use of things reusing, recycling, and reducing waste. Chemical solvents can be recycled if possible, unused equipment could be repaired and used again, and many items that were once considered for one time use can be replaced with reusable options. In fact, these methods not only fit in with eco-friendly practices, but this also reduces the amount of materials needed to be disposed of and the costs associated with that. Additionally, recycling materials used in forensic science can provide an efficient use of rare resources at the same time that the process is more environmentally minded. The aspect of moving from paper records to digital ledger technology is a significant part of sustainability in forensic science [15]. Then many of the conventional forensic methods are very much based on paper documentaries such as reports, evidence logs, and case files which cause deforestation and store a lot of spaces. A blockchain is used for recording forensics doing away with the need for paper and simplifying the documentation process. Information is captured, maintained in a secure and transparent publicized ledger while keeping private data secure, and authorized users have easy access to that information. Switching to digital formats is not just a viable way to cut on paper waste, but is a way to make case documentation more efficient, more precise and more accessible across all aspects of the workflows and long-term storage. Overall, merging blockchain and circular economy practices in forensic science shifts forward from sustainability into more of a technological upgrade [16].

Impact of a deteriorating environment on the results of forensic-based activities prompted the emergence of environment-protective policies and standards guiding the development of eco-friendly policies, regulations and standards limiting the environment effects and maintaining the quality and efficacy of forensic methods. These frameworks are the eco-friendly direction for forensic laboratories so that the field remains under the presence of the mutually sustainable goals of the world. However, in practical green forensic science, the rules and regulations are compulsory to follow green lab policy based on international standard (e.g. LEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design), ISO 14001), and certification program is needed for green lab based on international standard to specify the target which can be attained in an environment-friendly way. LEED certification, which has been recognized throughout the world, supplies the rules about developing, building, and producing energy efficient production, and many of the forensic laboratories are heading for the LEED certification. Minimize water consumption, guaranteeing the improved indoor environmental standards, and do it efficiently [17].

By contrast, ISO 14001 is about creating environmental management system, suggesting that laboratories consider environmental management which will help to reduce the waste, be efficient in terms of energy consumption, and control environmental pollution. Certifying these certifications gives forensic labs a demonstration of their commitment to sustainability while gaining the benefits of increased operational efficiency and less environmental footprint. Also, it is becoming increasingly relevant to have environmental policies in the domain of forensic research and criminal justice systems. Thus, these policies concentrate on making the forensic industry consult sustainable practices from legal institutions to residential institutions [18–20]. Now, there are now numerous countries throughout the world setting particular laws promising to minimize damage in forensic labs by having less of an impact on deadly chemicals, reusing resources and ending the disposal of wasteful materials.

10.5 Conclusion

On sustainable forensics, it is a huge shift when you talk about forensics cases, right where you have eco-friendly techniques or procedures, renewable energy options, AI, IoT, cloud computing, and blockchain to have minimal impacts on the environment. Traditional forensic uses have been energy-intensive practices, the use of hazardous chemicals, and the introduction of nondegradable materials. When blue chemistry principles are applied to forensic science by adopting digital forensic strategies, forensic science becomes a more sustainable and environment-friendly science field and blue chemistry principles and energy-efficient processes are used in forensic science laboratories.

A key aspect of this transformation lies in energy-efficient forensic laboratories that leverage smart grids, AI-driven automation, and IoT-based monitoring to optimize energy use. Renewable energy sources, such as solar, wind, and hydroelectric power, significantly reduce the carbon footprint of forensic labs while maintaining operational efficiency. Similarly, green forensic practices, such as biodegradable materials, digital documentation, and recycling programs, contribute to waste reduction without compromising investigative integrity. Advancements in cloud computing, edge computing and AI-based forensic automation led to the improvement in the efficiency and the reduction of the energy consumption in digital forensics. Thanks to the cloud-based forensic investigations we can access and store remote data without a need for physical infrastructure. In the case of blockchain technology, forensic evidence is further tamper-proof through the creation of tamper-free digital records that require less storage and less power usage to process.

While these innovations have provided solutions that are sustainable, the difficulties in the implementation of such solutions continue in the shape of huge initial costs, restriction of regulatory limitations, and resistance to change in traditional forensic institutions. Though, development of forensic labs towards ecological ones are becoming a necessity owing to the ever-increasing global concern for sustainability, environmental policies, certifications like ISO 14001, LEED, and green funding initiatives.

Forensic science must keep adopting innovative green technologies, international environmental standard, and energy-efficient method to ensure its long-term sustainability. It can enhance energy efficiency, improve environmental responsibility, and bring better results in investigative outcomes through the use of interdisciplinary collaboration among forensic experts, policymakers, and environmental scientists. Its future in the hands holds the promise to achieve the desired balance between the cutting edge of technology and environmental stewardship to construct a greener, more sustainable forensic future.
